Optimal Clustering of Discrete Mixtures: Binomial, Poisson, Block Models, and Multi-layer Networks
Zhongyuan Lyu, Ting Li, Dong Xia

Abstract
In this paper, we first study the fundamental limit of clustering networks when a multi-layer network is present. Under the mixture multi-layer stochastic block model (MMSBM), we show that the minimax optimal network clustering error rate, which takes an exponential form and is characterized by the Rényi- 1 / 2 1/2 divergence between the edge probability distributions of the component networks. We propose a novel two-stage network clustering method including a tensor-based initialization algorithm involving both node and sample splitting and a refinement procedure by likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm. Network clustering must be accompanied by node community detection. Our proposed algorithm achieves the minimax optimal network clustering error rate and allows extreme network sparsity under MMSBM. Numerical simulations and real data experiments both validate that our method outperforms existing methods. Oftentimes, the edges of networks carry count-type weights. We then extend our methodology and analysis framework to study the minimax optimal clustering error rate for mixture of discrete distributions including Binomial, Poisson, and multi-layer Poisson networks. The minimax optimal clustering error rates in these discrete mixtures all take the same exponential form characterized by the Rényi- 1 / 2 1/2 divergences. These optimal clustering error rates in discrete mixtures can also be achieved by our proposed two-stage clustering algorithm.



1
Introduction

Binary and count-type data routinely arise from diverse applications, especially in network data analysis. Unweighted networks have binary edges, encoding the existence or inexistence of a pairwise interaction between two nodes, e.g., whether Alice and Bob called each other during some week or whether there exists a direct flight between New York and Beijing. Networks can also have edges which carry count-type weight, e.g., the number of phone calls between Alice and Bob or number of direct flights between New York and Beijing. In the big data era, collections of networks have become increasingly accessible across various domains
(
Kivelä et al. 2014
;
Weber Zendrera et al. 2021
;
Li et al. 2021
)
. Typical examples include ecological networks
(
Poisot et al. 2016
)
representing the interactions of species in different ecosystems, trading networks
Lyu et al. 2021
;
Jing et al. 2021
of different types of commodities among countries, human brain connectivity networks derived from resting state functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI)
Li et al. 2021
;
Relión et al. 2019
;
Paul and Chen 2020a
.

Most of existing literature focused on community detection
(
Paul and Chen 2016
;
Paul and Chen 2020a
;
Chen et al. 2022
;
Agterberg et al. 2022
;
Jing et al. 2022
)
, which aims at clustering “similar” vertices into groups. When multiple networks are present, finding similarity between networks and clustering them into groups have great scientific values in many fields
(
Jing et al. 2021
)
. Basically, a network itself is viewed as a single unit of observation. Clustering networks aims at discriminating samples on the graph level, aids comparative and targeted research, reduces redundancy for increased efficiency, and unveils concealed structural information within each layer of network.

Broadly speaking, two types of network clustering methods have appeared in the literature. The first type consists of those graphon-based methods
(
Mukherjee et al. 2017
;
Sabanayagam et al. 2021
;
Shao et al. 2022
)
. These methods are non-parametric in nature, offering great practical flexibility in that network sizes can vary and node registration can be absent. The ultimate clustering is performed on a low-dimensional embedding of networks. Nonetheless, it is unclear what the “optimal” embedding is in practice and the non-parametric framework lacks interpretability of the results of network clustering. The second line of methods are model-based, assuming that the observed networks are generated by some mixture model. See, e.g.,
Stanley et al. 2016
;
Signorelli and Wit 2020
;
Mantziou et al. 2021
;
Chabert-Liddell et al. 2022
;
Rebafka 2023
. Model-based approaches facilitate the design of specially crafted clustering algorithms and enjoy better interpretability. However, the forging works provided few insights on the theoretical fronts of network clustering. More recently,
Jing et al. 2021
proposed a mixture multi-layer stochastic block model (MMSBM), which simultaneously models node communities and network clusters. It is a generalization of SBM
(
Holland et al. 1983
)
- one of the most popular generative models for a single network. MMSBM allows heterogeneous node community assignments across different networks. A tensor-based clustering algorithm was designed in
Jing et al. 2021
, which can consistently recover the node communities and network clusters even when the observed networks are extremely sparse. Some other works
Fan et al. 2021
;
Pensky and Wang 2021
proposed alternative algorithms attempting to achieve smaller clustering error but with a more stringent network sparsity condition.
Chen et al. 2022
studied a generative multi-layer network models and investigated the optimal error rates in node community detection. Their proposed model is free of network labels so that network clustering was not an essential goal.

This paper aims at studying the fundamental limit of clustering networks, e.g., what is the optimal clustering error and what quantity plays the essential role there. The question is challenging for several reasons. First of all, it seems that network clustering must be accompanied by node community detection. Without taking advantage of node community information, multiple networks are barely matrix-valued observations with binary entries where clustering becomes extremely challenging if only some low-rank structure is present, even for low-rank Gaussian mixture model
(
Lyu and Xia 2022
)
. Optimal network clustering under MMSBM thus hinges upon efficient node community detection. Secondly, multi-layer networks can be very sparse, for example, some network may even be a dis-connected graph. Intuitively, it can be statistically challenging to compare different networks if most of them are dis-connected graphs. We shall design novel clustering methods and technical tools to cope with the extreme sparsity in multi-layer networks. Lastly, model-based clustering methods usually require specific design of computational algorithms for both initialization and node community detection. The integrated analysis of both computational and statistical performances is also technically challenging.

By a careful inspection of the oracle case and a comparable minimax lower bound, we find that the minimax optimal error rate of clustering networks under MMSBM takes the form
\exp(-I^{\ast}/2)
as
I^{\ast}\to\infty
. Here
I^{\ast}
represents the Rényi-
1/2
divergence
(
Van Erven and Harremos 2014
)
between the two Bernoulli random matrices characterizing the edge generating mechanism of two SBMs. Rényi divergence measures the “distance” between probability distributions, for example, the Rényi-
1/2
divergence between
{\rm Bern}(p)
and
{\rm Bern}(q)
is simply
-2\log\big((pq)^{1/2}+(1-p)^{1/2}(1-q)^{1/2}\big)
; the Rényi-
1/2
divergence between
{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{1})
and
{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{2})
is
\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}-2\theta_{1}^{1/2}\theta_{2}^{1/2}
. The formal definition of
I^{\ast}
under MMSBM can be found in Section
2
. One can simply view
I^{\ast}
as the separation strength in mixture models. We first show that the exponential-type rate
\exp(-I^{\ast}/2)
is achievable by the oracle version of a likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm assuming that the SBM parameters are known. A matching minimax lower bound is also established confirming the derived rate is optimal. This significantly improves those sub-optimal polynomial-type error rates attained by prior works
(
Jing et al. 2021
;
Fan et al. 2021
;
Pensky and Wang 2021
)
, thereby filling the void in the optimal error rate of network clustering under MMSBM. More importantly, we propose a novel two-stage clustering algorithm based on the popular initialization then refinement scheme. The algorithm begins with initial node community detection and layer clustering, which are utilized to estimate the edge probability matrices. The refinement step re-assigns layer label to networks based on the maximum likelihood. The accuracy of estimated edge probability matrices crucially affects the success of refinement procedure. We prove that the two-stage clustering algorithm can achieve the minimax optimal network clustering error rate, both in expectation and with high probability, as long as the initial node community detection and layer clustering are sufficiently accurate.

It thus remains to design an efficient initial node community detection and layer clustering algorithm under MMSBM. Unfortunately, the tensor-based spectral method proposed in
Jing et al. 2021
, while being able to deliver a consistent clustering for both nodes and layers, cannot provide a sufficiently accurate initial clustering of nodes or layers. Due to technical reasons, the clustering error rate delivered by
Jing et al. 2021
involves additional
\log n
terms and, as a result, our newly proposed two-stage clustering algorithm works only if the separation strength
I^{\ast}\gg\log n
, which actually implies exact clustering in view of the optimal rate
\exp(-I^{\ast}/2)
. Here
n
denotes the number of layers. We design a novel initialization algorithm based on tensor decomposition and node and sample switching. The algorithm involves both node and network sample splitting and uses two sub-samples for cross spectral estimate. The sample splitting allows us to derive a sharp concentration inequality for the sum of random sparse matrices. Note that sample splitting introduces loss of efficiency only for initialization, which is recovered subsequently by the refinement procedure. By combining the initialization and two-stage clustering algorithms, we end up with a computationally efficient method which achieves minimax optimal network clustering error rate and allows extreme network sparsity under MMSBM. Besides the theoretical achievements, numerical simulations and real data experiments demonstrate that our new network clustering algorithm considerably outperforms the method proposed in
Jing et al. 2021
.

It is noteworthy that the exponential-type minimax optimal clustering error rate has also been found in other problems. Notable examples include node community detection in a single network
(
Zhang and Zhou 2016
;
Gao et al. 2017
)
and multi-layer networks
(
Chen et al. 2022
)
, and clustering task for Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM)
(
Löffler et al. 2019
;
Löffler et al. 2020
)
under the assumption of isotropic covariance. In particular,
Gao et al. 2017
designed a computationally feasible two-stage algorithm, consisting of a spectral initialization and a refinement procedure motivated by penalized MLE, which achieves the optimal node clustering error rate under SBM. More exactly, the optimal rate takes the form
\exp(-dI_{0}/2)
where
d
is the number of nodes and
I_{0}
denotes the Rényi-
1/2
divergence between
{\rm Bern}(p)
and
{\rm Bern}(q)
. Here
p
and
q
represents the within-community and between-community edge probabilities, respectively. On the other hand, the seminal work
Lu and Zhou 2016
first derived the optimal clustering error rate in GMM, taking the form
\exp(-\Delta^{2}/8)
, for the widely-used Lloyd’s algorithm
Lloyd 1982
, where the separation strength
\Delta
denotes the Frobenius norm of the difference of two population center matrices. The same rate was later established by
Gao and Zhang 2022
under a more general framework with a weaker separation condition on
\Delta
. It has also been shown that spectral clustering can achieve optimal rates in GMM
(
Löffler et al. 2019
;
Zhang and Zhou 2022
)
and SBM
(
Zhang 2023
)
. More recently,
Lyu and Xia 2022
introduced a low-rank mixture model (LrMM) for clustering matrix-valued observations and proposed a low-rank Lloyd’s algorithm to leverage the planted structures within data. Their method also achieves an optimal clustering error rate in the same exponential form.

We also extend MMSBM to multi-layer weighted networks where edges can carry count-type weights. The model is referred to as the mixture multi-layer Poisson block model (MMPBM) in that the edge-wise weight follows a Poisson distribution. Poisson block model has been studied for weighted networks. See, e.g.,
Sewell and Chen 2016
;
Chiquet et al. 2019
.
By analyzing the oracle case and a likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm, we show that the minimax optimal rate of network clustering under MMPBM also takes the form
\exp(-I^{\ast}/2)
where
I^{\ast}
is the Rényi-
1/2
divergence between the Poisson distributions describing the edge weight distribution. A similar two-stage clustering algorithm is shown to achieve the minimax optimal clustering error rate if well initialized. Finally, we study the optimal clustering of other discrete mixtures including the mixture of Binomial distributions
(
Blischke 1964
)
and the mixture of Poisson distributions
(
Karlis and Xekalaki 2005
)
.

Our contributions are summarized as follows. We study the clustering problem and investigate the fundamental limits for several mixture models of discrete distributions including Binomial, Poisson, multi-layer binary networks, and multi-layer Poisson networks. By analyzing a likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm and establishing respective minimax lower bounds, we demonstrate that the minimax optimal clustering error rate in these discrete mixtures takes a universal exponential form where the exponent reflects the Rényi-
1/2
divergence between the underlying discrete distributions. To our best knowledge, these are the first results revealing the fundamental limits of clustering error rate in discrete mixtures. In particular, layer clustering error rate is of great interest in multi-layer networks analysis. We provide a general two-stage clustering algorithm based on the initialization-then-refinement scheme which achieves the minimax optimal clustering error rate under the aforementioned discrete mixture models. Initialization under MMSBM is challenging. We design a novel initialization algorithm involving both node and sample switching and cross spectral estimate, which is guaranteed to provide a sufficiently accurate initialization even when the multi-layer networks are extremely sparse.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we study the MMSBM for multi-layer binary networks, derive the minimax lower bound of network clustering error rate, present a two-stage clustering algorithm achieving the minimax optimal network clustering error rate, and introduce a novel initialization algorithm based on node and sample splitting. Section
3
focuses on the MMPBM for multi-layer weighted networks when edges carry Poisson-type weights. The minimax lower bound of network clustering error rate is developed and we show that the two-stage clustering algorithm is able to achieve the optimal clustering error rate under MMPBM. Mixture of Binomial distributions and Poisson distributions is investigated in Section
4
. Numerical simulations and real data experiments are presented in Section
5
, which validate the theoretical findings of this paper. All proofs and technical lemmas are deferred to the Appendix.

The following common notations will frequently appear in later sections. We use
c,c_{0},c_{1}\cdots
and
C,C_{0},C_{1},\cdots
to denote generic small and large constants, which may vary from line to line. For nonnegative sequences
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
, we write
x_{n}\lesssim y_{n}
or
y_{n}\gtrsim x_{n}
or
x_{n}=O(y_{n})
or
y_{n}=\Omega(x_{n})
if there exists a constant
C>0
such that
x_{n}\leq Cy_{n}
, write
x_{n}\asymp y_{n}
if
x_{n}\lesssim y_{n}
and
y_{n}\lesssim x_{n}
, and write
x_{n}\ll y_{n}
or
y_{n}\gg x_{n}
or
x_{n}=o(y_{n})
or
y_{n}=\omega(x_{n})
if
x_{n}=O(c_{n}y_{n})
for some
c_{n}\rightarrow 0
. Denote
\left\|\cdot\right\|
the
\ell_{2}
-norm for vectors and operator norms for matrices. Let
\|\cdot\|_{\rm F}
denote the matrix Frobenious norm.

2
Optimal Clustering of Mixture Multi-layer Networks

2.1
Mixture multi-layer stochastic block model

The mixture multi-layer stochastic block model (MMSBM) was introduced in
Jing et al. 2021
for community detection in multi-layer networks. Suppose we observe a collection of undirected, binary networks
\{\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of common
d
nodes, or equivalently, their symmetric adjacency matrices
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d},i\in[n]\}
. The essential assumption is that each network, a so-called
layer
, is sampled independently from one of two
1
1

1

For simplicity, we focus on a two-component mixture model, but the results can be easily extended to multi-component cases.
stochastic block model (SBM). Let a vector
\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\in[2]^{n}
denote the latent label of each network, i.e.,
z^{\ast}_{i}=2
meaning that the
i
-th network was sampled from the second SBM. The two SBM’s are characterized by two
community probability matrices

\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2}\in(0,1)^{K\times K}
and
community assignment

\bm{\sigma}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{2}:[d]\mapsto[K]
. Here we assume there are
K
communities in each SBM for simplicity. Each edge of the network is independently sampled as

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{i}(j_{1},j_{2})\sim\text{Bernoulli}\left(\mathbf{B}_{z^{\ast}_{i}}(\bm{\sigma}_{z^{\ast}_{i}}(j_{1}),\bm{\sigma}_{z^{\ast}_{i}}(j_{2}))\right),\quad i\in[n],\ j_{1},j_{2}\in[d]

Note that we allow self-loops for notational simplicity, whereas there is no essential difficulty in extending our results to the scenario with no self-loops.

After observing all the networks
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{i},i\in[n]
, our goal is to cluster them into two groups, i.e., which layers are possibly generated from the same SBM.
The Hamming distance evaluates the goodness of clustering:

h(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=\min_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}(z_{i}\neq\pi(z^{\ast}_{i})),

(1)

where
\mathbf{z}
is a vector in
[2]^{n}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
stands for the set of all permutations of
[k]
. It can be viewed as the mis-clustering error of multi-layer networks. Previously,
Jing et al. 2021
proposed a tensor-decomposition based approach for layer clustering and showed that it can consistently recover the layer labels. Our interest is to characterize the minimax optimal clustering error under MMSBM. For ease of exposition, define
{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z}^{\ast}):=\{i\in[n]:z^{\ast}_{i}=m\}
and
S_{k}(\bm{\sigma}_{m}):=\{j\in[d]:\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j)=k\}
as the corresponding index set of
\mathbf{z}^{\ast}
and
\bm{\sigma}_{m}
for
\forall m\in[2],k\in[K]
. Denote
n_{m}:=|{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z}^{\ast})|
so that
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
.

Throughout this section, we focus on the following parameter space of MMSBM:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Theta}:=\mathbf{\Theta}(n,d,K,\mathbf{p},\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\bigg\{\left(\mathbf{z},\{\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2}\},\{\bm{\sigma}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{2}\}\right):\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n},|{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z})|\in\left[\frac{n}{2\alpha},\frac{\alpha n}{2}\right],

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}_{m}:[d]\rightarrow[K],|{\cal S}_{k}(\bm{\sigma}_{m})|\in\left[\frac{d}{\beta K},\frac{\beta d}{K}\right],\mathbf{B}_{m}=\mathbf{B}_{m}^{\top}\in(0,1)^{K\times K},

\displaystyle p_{m}:=\min_{i}\mathbf{B}_{m}(i,i)\geq\gamma^{-1}\max_{i}\mathbf{B}_{m}(i,i),\ \gamma\min_{i\neq j}\mathbf{B}_{m}(i,j)\geq q_{m}:=\max_{i\neq j}\mathbf{B}_{m}(i,j),

\displaystyle\max\{p_{m_{1}}/p_{m_{2}},q_{m_{1}}/q_{m_{2}},p_{m_{1}}/q_{m_{2}},q_{m_{1}}/p_{m_{2}}\}\leq\gamma,\ \forall m_{1},m_{2}\in[2]\bigg\},

(2)

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\geq 1
are assumed to be absolute constants and
\mathbf{p}:=(p_{1},q_{1},p_{2},q_{2})
is a vector containing the boundary probabilities. Denote
\mathbf{P}_{m}(j_{1},j_{2}):=\mathbf{B}_{m}\left(\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j_{1}),\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j_{2})\right)
the edge probability matrix, i.e., the expectation of adjacency matrix under SBM
\left(\mathbf{B}_{m},\bm{\sigma}_{m}\right)
. Note that the network sparsity is characterized by the probabilities
p_{m},q_{m}
and we will be particularly interested in the extremely sparse case when
p_{m},q_{m}
are of order
((n\wedge d)d)^{-1}
.

Since network clustering becomes trivial if the two probability matrices
\mathbf{P}_{m_{1}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{m_{2}}
are strikingly different, we focus on the more difficult yet challenging regime where the edge probabilities are homogeneous, i.e.,
p_{m_{1}}/p_{m_{2}}
and
q_{m_{1}}/q_{m_{2}}
are bounded for
\forall m_{1},m_{2}\in[2]
.
Homogeneous probability condition as above is typical in existing literature of studying minimax rates in network analysis. See, e.g.,
Zhang and Zhou 2020
;
Zhou and Li 2018
;
Gao et al. 2017
;
Chen et al. 2022
;
Zhen et al.
;
Fei and Chen 2019
;
Zhang 2023
.

2.2
Oracle property of likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm

Lloyd’s algorithm
(
Lloyd 1982
)
is a simple yet popular clustering algorithm. The algorithm alternates between re-estimating the cluster centers and re-assigning cluster labels. It has been demonstrated that Lloyd’s algorithm can achieve statistically optimal clustering error under various mixture models such as GMM
(
Lu and Zhou 2016
)
and the low-rank mixture model (LrMM,
Lyu and Xia 2022
;
Lyu and Xia 2023
).

In this section, we explore the limit of Lloyd’s algorithm under MMSBM by considering the oracle case. Under Gaussian mixture model, the oracle case means the situation where the population cluster centers are known beforehand and the label of a new observation can be decided by comparing its distances from the known population cluster centers. Similarly, for MMSBM, oracle case refers to the situation that the edge probability matrices
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
are already given. If an observed network
\mathbf{X}\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d}
is known sampled from either
\mathbf{P}_{1}
or
\mathbf{P}_{2}
, the log-likelihood can be written as

\displaystyle\ell(\mathbf{X}|z):=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\mathbf{P}_{z}(\omega)+\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}(1-\mathbf{X}(\omega))\log\left(1-\mathbf{P}_{z}(\omega)\right)

where
{\cal J}_{d}:=\{(j_{1},j_{2}):1\leq j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq d\}
and
z
takes values in
\{1,2\}
. The likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm then assigns the label to
\mathbf{X}
which maximizes the log-likelihood
\ell(\mathbf{X}|z)
, i.e.,
\widehat{z}:=\arg\min_{z\in\{1,2\}}\ell(\mathbf{X}|z)
.

Without loss of generality, assume the true label of
\mathbf{X}
is
1
. This network is mis-clustered by Lloyd’s algorithm if

\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}.

(3)

By studying the probability of event defined in (
3
), we get the oracle mis-clustering error rate of Lloyd’s algorithm, which is formally stated in the following lemma. Note that the result holds for any given edge probability matrices, which are not necessarily from stochastic block models.

Lemma 1
.

Suppose that the edge probability matrices
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
are known, and a new observed network
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from the probability matrix
\mathbf{P}_{1}
., i.e.,
\mathbf{X}(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ind.}}}{{\sim}}{\rm Bern}(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))
for
\forall\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}
. The probability of mis-clustering
\mathbf{X}
by Lloyd’s algorithm is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{X}\textrm{ is mis-clustered})\leq\exp\left(-I^{*}/2\right),

where
I^{*}=-2\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\big(\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}+\sqrt{(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}\big)
. Similarly, the same bound holds if
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from the probability matrix
\mathbf{P}_{2}
.

The
Rényi divergence
of order
1/2
between two distributions
{\rm Bern}(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))
and
{\rm Bern}(\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))
is known as
I_{\omega}:=-2\log\big(\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}+\sqrt{(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}\big)
, implying that the exponent appeared in Lemma
1
can be written as
I^{\ast}=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}I_{\omega}
. Hereafter,
I^{\ast}
is regarded as the
separation strength
under MMSBM. The Rényi divergence has commonly played the role of SNR in literature of network community detection
Zhang and Zhou 2016
;
Gao et al. 2017
. For instance, the optimal mis-clustering rate for community detection in a two-community single SBM whose within-clustering and between-cluster edge probabilities are
p
and
q
, respectively, is

\exp\big(-(1-o(1))dI_{0}/2\big),

where
I_{0}:=-2\log\left(\sqrt{pq}+\sqrt{1-p}\sqrt{1-q}\right)
. It implies that a consistent clustering requires SNR condition
dI_{0}\rightarrow\infty
. Lemma
1
shows that a necessary condition of consistently clustering multiple networks under MMSBM is
I^{\ast}\rightarrow\infty
.

Comparison with Gaussian mixture model

For simplicity, consider the two-component Gaussian mixture model
\frac{1}{2}N\big({\rm vec}(\mathbf{P}_{1}),\sigma\mathbf{I}\big)+\frac{1}{2}N\big({\rm vec}(\mathbf{P}_{2}),\sigma\mathbf{I}\big)
where the population cluster centers are
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
, respectively. The difficulty of clustering is essentially characterized by the separation strength:

\Delta:=\|\mathbf{P}_{1}-\mathbf{P}_{2}\|_{\rm F}.

It has been shown that
(
Zhang and Zhou 2022
;
Lu and Zhou 2016
;
Löffler et al. 2019
;
Lyu and Xia 2022
)
the minimax optimal mis-clustering error rate is
\exp\big(-(1-o(1))\Delta^{2}/(8\sigma^{2})\big)
. In particular, this minimax rate holds for GMM
(
Löffler et al. 2019
)
, sparse GMM (i.e.,
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
are sparse,
Löffler et al. 2020
), and low-rank GMM (i.e.,
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
are low-rank,
Lyu and Xia 2022
). Furthermore,
Lyu and Xia 2022
showed that a Lloyd’s algorithm achieves the same rate even if the noise is sub-Gaussian with a variance proxy parameter
\sigma^{2}_{\textsf{sg}}
. Under MMSBM, the noise is Bernoulli and thus sub-Gaussian. If
p_{m},q_{m}\asymp 1
, then
\sigma_{\textsf{sg}}\asymp 1
and the exponent
\Delta^{2}/\sigma_{\textsf{sg}}^{2}\asymp\|\mathbf{P}_{1}-\mathbf{P}_{2}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\asymp I^{\ast}
. Together with Lemma
1
, this implies that the low-rank Lloyd’s algorithm proposed in
Lyu and Xia 2022
can achieve minimax optimal clustering error rate under MMSBM if the edge probabilities are bounded away from zero. However, when
p_{m},q_{m}=o(1)
(the most interesting scenario for network analysis), the variance proxy parameter
\sigma_{\textsf{sg}}\asymp 1

(
Arbel et al. 2020
)
so that
\Delta^{2}/(8\sigma_{\textsf{sg}}^{2})\asymp\Delta^{2}
. The exponent in Lemma
1
becomes
I^{\ast}\asymp\sum_{\omega}(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))^{2}\big(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\vee\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\big)^{-1}\gg\Delta^{2}
. This suggests that the low-rank Lloyd’s algorithm fails to achieve the ideal error rate stated in Lemma
1
when clustering sparse networks. The reason is that the low-rank Lloyd’s algorithm essentially finds the local MLE solution assuming the Gaussian noise. Consequently, the Euclidean distance is no longer an optimal criterion under MMSBM because of the difference of likelihood functions between Bernoulli and Gaussian distributions. Such sub-optimality has been demonstrated for spectral clustering on non-Gaussian data in
Zhang and Zhou 2022
.

2.3
Minimax lower bound

We now characterize the minimax lower bound of clustering networks under MMSBM.
Recall the working parameter space
\mathbf{\Theta}(n,d,K,\mathbf{p},\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
and Hamming distance
h(\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{z}_{2})
defined in (
2.1
) and (
1
), respectively. It actually suffices to consider a fix
(\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2},\bm{\sigma}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{2})
that satisfies the constraint in (
2.1
). Denote
\mathbf{B}_{1,2}:=\{\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2}\}
,
\bm{\sigma}_{1,2}:=\{\bm{\sigma}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{2}\}
, and

\mathbf{\Theta}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\ \bm{\sigma}_{1,2})}:=\big\{\mathbf{z}:\ \big(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2}\big)\textrm{ satisfies constraints in }\eqref{eq:parameter-space}\big\}

The minimax lower bound in the following theorem presents an exponential rate, which is concordant with the oracle mis-clustering rate in Lemma
1
.

Theorem 1
.

For fixed
\mathbf{B}_{1,2}
and
\bm{\sigma}_{1,2}
satisfying the constraints in (
2.1
), let
\mathbf{P}_{1}=\mathbf{B}_{1}\circ\bm{\sigma}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}=\mathbf{B}_{2}\circ\bm{\sigma}_{2}
, and define

I^{\ast}:=I^{\ast}(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2}):=-2\sum\nolimits_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\Big(\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}+\sqrt{(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}\Big)

(4)

If
I^{\ast}\to\infty
, then

\inf_{\widehat{}\mathbf{z}}\ \sup_{\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\in\mathbf{\Theta}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})}}\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\geq\exp\left(-(1+o(1))\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\right),

where the infimum is taken over all possible clustering algorithms working on the multi-layer networks sampled from MMSBM with parameters
(\mathbf{z}^{\ast},\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})
.

2.4
Achieving optimal clustering via a two-stage algorithm

In
Jing et al. 2021
, a spectral clustering algorithm was proposed for layer clustering under MMSBM based on tensor decomposition. The algorithm only achieves consistency and the attained mis-clustering error rate decays polynomially, which is sub-optimal in view of the established minimax lower bound in Theorem
1
. This sub-optimality is essentially caused by the fact that tensor decomposition finds MLE of the network model using Gaussian likelihood functions.

Lemma
1
suggests that optimal clustering error rate can be achieved if the edge probability matrices
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
are known. In practice, these matrices must be estimated from the observed networks and a clustering algorithm tends to achieve smaller mis-clustering error if the edge probability matrices are estimated more accurately. The following lemma characterizes the desired accuracy of estimated edge probability matrices that ensures the likelihood-based method achieves the minimax optimal clustering error rate. Denote the
\ell_{1}
-norm
\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\ell_{1}}:=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}|\mathbf{P}(\omega)|
for a symmetric matrix
\mathbf{P}
and sup-norm
\|\mathbf{P}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}:=\max_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}|\mathbf{P}(\omega)|
. We emphasize that, as in Lemma
1
, the established bound in Lemma
2
holds without assuming block structures on
\mathbf{P}_{1}
or
\mathbf{P}_{2}
. Basically, these edge probability matrices can be generic as long as the estimated matrices are sufficiently accurate with respect to the
\ell_{1}
-norm.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that a network
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from the edge probability matrix
\mathbf{P}_{1}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{X}(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ind.}}}{{\sim}}{\rm Bern}(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))
for
\forall\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}
. Let
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}
be the edge probability matrices estimated without using
\mathbf{X}
. Let
\widehat{z}
be the MLE of
\mathbf{X}
’s label based on
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}
:

\widehat{z}:=\underset{z\in\{1,2\}}{\arg\max}\ \sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{z}(\omega)+\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\big(1-\mathbf{X}(\omega)\big)\log\big(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{z}(\omega)\big)

(5)

If
\|\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}-\mathbf{P}_{1}\|_{\ell_{1}},\|\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}-\mathbf{P}_{2}\|_{\ell_{1}}=o(I^{\ast})
where
I^{\ast}
is as defined in Lemma
1
and
I^{\ast}\to\infty
, then, conditioned on
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{z}=2)\leq\exp\bigg(-\big(1-o(1)\big)\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\bigg)

The same bound still holds if
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from the probability matrix
\mathbf{P}_{2}
.

The implications of Lemma
2
for network clustering under MMSBM are two-fold. First, a network
\mathbf{X}_{i}
can be clustered/classified accurately with a minimal error rate if the unknown edge probability matrices can be estimated accurately. Second, the estimated edge probability matrices should be independent with the network
\mathbf{X}_{i}
. The independence can be achieved easily by leaving
\mathbf{X}_{i}
out, i.e., estimating the edge probability matrices using networks
\mathbf{X}_{j}
’s,
j\in[n]\setminus i
. However, the challenging task is to obtain estimated edge probability matrices which are sufficiently accurate in sup-norm distance. Suppose that all the networks
\mathbf{X}_{j}
’s are correctly clustered so that
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}
are attained by the sample averages of networks within each cluster, respectively, Chernoff bound tells us that
\|\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}-\mathbf{P}_{1}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}
is, with high probability, approximately in the order
O(\sqrt{p_{1}/n})
. This is exceedingly larger than than the desired accuracy
O(I^{\ast}d^{-2})
unless the networks are dense, more more precisely, the network sparsity should satisfy
np_{1}=\Omega(1)
.

The block structures of
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
under MMSBM facilitate more accurate estimations. Basically, the entries of
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
can now take the average not only from the multiple networks but also from observed entries within each network. The accuracy of estimated edge probability matrices then rely on both the clustering accuracy of networks and community detection accuracy of nodes, which can be achieved by any initial clustering algorithm as long as it can consistently recover the network clusters and node communities, e.g., the tensor-based initial clustering algorithm in
Jing et al. 2021
or a variant we shall introduce later.

Our methods thus consists of two stages. The first step aims to estimate the edge probability matrices. In order to accurately cluster the network
\mathbf{X}_{i}
, we apply an initial clustering and community detection algorithm to the other networks
\{\mathbf{X}_{j}:j\in[n]\setminus i\}
, which outputs an initial estimated layer labels
\tilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}\in[2]^{n-1}
and node community memberships
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{1}^{(-i)},\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{2}^{(-i)}:[d]\mapsto[K]
. Here
K
stands for the number of communities in each SBM. Based on these initial layer labels and node community memberships, we estimate the edge probability matrices, denoted by
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}^{(-i)}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}^{(-i)}
. They are independent of the network
\mathbf{X}_{i}
.
The second stage of our algorithm then assigns a label to
\mathbf{X}_{i}
using the MLE approach as in (
5
). Lemma
2
dictates that the estimated label of
\mathbf{X}_{i}
is correct with a minimax optimal probability guarantee. The second stage thus provides a refined estimate of the label of
\mathbf{X}_{i}
. At a high level, our method follows the popular initialization then refinement scheme in studying minimax optimal community detection in network analysis
(
Lu and Zhou 2016
;
Gao et al. 2017
;
Chen et al. 2022
;
Gao and Zhang 2022
)
. The detailed implementation of our method can be found in Algorithm
1
. For ease of exposition, with slight abuse of notation, we now denote
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{(-i)}
as an
n
-dimensional vector by adding a dummy entry
\tilde{z}^{(-i)}_{i}=0
. The last step of Algorithm
1
serves to re-align the estimated layer labels to eliminate the permutation issue, inspired by
Gao et al. 2017
.

Data:

network samples
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
K
-the number of communities in each network,
\text{Init}(\cdot)
-an initialization algorithm for estimating network labels and local community memberships.

Result:

network label
\widehat{}\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n}

for

i\in[n]

do

1

(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)},\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{1}^{(-i)},\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{2}^{(-i)})=\text{Init}(\{\mathbf{X}_{i^{\prime}}\}_{i^{\prime}\neq i})

2

for

m\in\{1,2\}

do

3

for

k,l\in[K]

do

4

\displaystyle\widehat{}\mathbf{B}^{(-i)}_{m}(k,l)=\frac{\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}^{n}\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{z}_{i^{\prime}}^{(-i)}=m)\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}_{i^{\prime}}(\omega)\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega)=(k,l))}{\sum_{i^{\prime}\neq i}^{n}\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{z}_{i^{\prime}}^{(-i)}=m)\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega)=(k,l))}

(6)

end for

5

end for

6

\widehat{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=\widetilde{z}_{j}^{(-i)}
for all
j\neq i\in[n]
and

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}=\argmax_{m\in[2]}\Bigg[

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega\in{\cal J}_{d})\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega)}+\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\left(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega)\right)\Bigg]

(7)

where
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega):=\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{m}^{(-i)}\left(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega)\right)

end for

7

Let
\widehat{z}_{1}=\widehat{z}_{1}^{(-1)}
.
for

i=2

to

n

do

8

\widehat{z}_{i}=\argmax_{m\in\{1,2\}}\left|\left\{j\in[n]:\widehat{z}_{j}^{(-1)}=m\right\}\bigcap\left\{j\in[n]:\widehat{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}\right\}\right|

end for

9

Algorithm 1

Two-stage network clustering under MMSBM

By Lemma
2
, the accuracy of the estimated layer labels
\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)},i\in[n]
is essentially determined by the precision of the estimated edge probability matrices, which relies on the effectiveness of the initialization algorithm in finding the layer labels and node community memberships. Suppose that the initialization algorithm ensures

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big\{\max_{i\in[n]}h(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{(-i)},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\eta_{z}\Big\}\ \bigcap\Big\{\max_{i\in[n];i\in[2]}h(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}^{(-i)},\bm{\sigma}_{m})\leq\eta_{\sigma}\Big\}\Big)\geq 1-C_{1}n^{-2}

(8)

for some absolute constant
C_{1}>0
. Here
\eta_{z},\eta_{\sigma}\to 0
as
n,d\to\infty
and they reflect the effectiveness of the initialization algorithm. Note that the hamming distance
h(\bm{\sigma}_{1},\widehat{\bm{\sigma}}_{1})
between any local community memberships is defined in the same fashion as in (
1
).

The performance of both the initialization algorithm and Algorithm
1
crucially relies on the network sparsity. We primarily focus on the extreme sparse case and set

p_{1}:=\frac{a_{1}}{(n\wedge d)d},\quad p_{2}:=\frac{a_{2}}{(n\wedge d)d},\quad q_{1}:=\frac{b_{1}}{(n\wedge d)d},\quad{\rm and}\quad q_{2}:=\frac{b_{2}}{(n\wedge d)d}

(9)

Denote
\bar{a}:=(a_{1}\vee a_{2})
and
\underline{a}:=(a_{1}\wedge a_{2})
. The following theorem affirms that Algorithm
1
achieves the minimax optimal clustering error rate. Recall that
K
denotes the number of communities in each SBM.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose that the initialization algorithm satisfies (
8
) with
\eta_{\sigma}=O\big(\min_{m\in[2]}(a_{m}-b_{m})/(a_{m}K)\big)
,

\eta_{\sigma}\log\frac{1}{\eta_{\sigma}}=o\bigg(\frac{I^{*2}}{\bar{a}K^{2}(1+dn^{-1})^{2}}\bigg)\quad{\rm and}\quad\eta_{\sigma}\vee\eta_{z}=o\bigg(\frac{I^{\ast}}{\bar{a}K^{2}(1+dn^{-1})}\bigg)

(10)

If
\underline{a}\geq C
for some absolute constants
C>0
,
K^{2}\log n=o(n)
, and
I^{\ast}\to\infty
,
then, conditioned on the event (
8
), with probability at least
1-\exp\left(-(I^{*})^{1-\epsilon}\right)
for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, the output of Algorithm
1
satisfies

h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\Big(-(1-o(1))\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\Big).

Moreover,

\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\Big(-(1-o(1))\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\Big)+O\left(n^{-C^{\prime}}\right),

and some large constant
C^{\prime}>0
.

Let us illustrate the implications of Theorem
3
for special cases. The case of particular interest is
p_{m},q_{m}=o(1)
so that

\displaystyle I^{\ast}\asymp\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\frac{\big(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\big)^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\vee\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\asymp

\displaystyle\frac{(p_{1}-p_{2})^{2}}{p_{1}\vee p_{2}}\cdot\frac{d^{2}}{K}+\frac{(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}}{q_{1}\vee q_{2}}\cdot d^{2}

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle\frac{(a_{1}-a_{2})^{2}}{K(a_{1}\vee a_{2})}\Big(1+\frac{d}{n}\big)+\frac{(b_{1}-b_{2})^{2}}{b_{1}\vee b_{2}}\Big(1+\frac{d}{n}\Big),

if we further assume that the local community memberships are the same, i.e.,
\bm{\sigma}_{1}=\bm{\sigma}_{2}
. Assume
K=O(1)
for simplicity, the condition
I^{\ast}\to\infty
holds as long as

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{(a_{1}-a_{2})^{2}}{a_{1}\vee a_{2}}+\frac{(b_{1}-b_{2})^{2}}{b_{1}\vee b_{2}}\bigg)\bigg(1+\frac{d}{n}\bigg)\to\infty\quad{\rm as}\quad n,d\to\infty.

(11)

and the mis-clustering error rate becomes

\displaystyle h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\bigg\{-\Omega\Big(\frac{(a_{1}-a_{2})^{2}}{a_{1}\vee a_{2}}+\frac{(b_{1}-b_{2})^{2}}{b_{1}\vee b_{2}}\Big)\cdot\Big(1+\frac{d}{n}\Big)\bigg\}

(12)

The sparsity conditions (
11
) and
\underline{a}=\Omega(1)
are much weaker than that is required in
Jing et al. 2021
, which reads
\underline{a}=\Omega\big(\log^{4}(n\vee d)\big)
. The bound (
12
) implies that networks can be more accurately clustered if network sizes grow, which is reasonable since it facilitates more precise estimation of edge probability matrices. Moreover, exact network clustering is possible if

\Big(\frac{(a_{1}-a_{2})^{2}}{a_{1}\vee a_{2}}+\frac{(b_{1}-b_{2})^{2}}{b_{1}\vee b_{2}}\Big)\cdot\Big(1+\frac{d}{n}\Big)=\Omega(\log n),

which is also much weaker than the conditions required by
Jing et al. 2021
.

The initialization requirement in the aforementioned case is also inspiring. Note that
-x\log x=o(x^{1/(1+\delta)})
as
x\to 0
for any constant
\delta>0
. For the sake of clarity, assume that
(b_{1}-b_{2})^{2}/(b_{1}\vee b_{2})\asymp(a_{1}-a_{2})^{2}/(a_{1}\vee a_{2})
. Then, the initialization condition (
10
) is equivalent to

\eta_{\sigma}=o\bigg(\frac{1}{\bar{a}^{1+\delta}}\cdot\Big(\frac{(a_{1}-a_{2})^{2}}{\bar{a}}\Big)^{2(1+\delta)}\bigg)

for any
\delta>0
and

\eta_{\sigma},\eta_{z}=o\bigg(\frac{1}{\bar{a}}\cdot\Big(\frac{(a_{1}-a_{2})^{2}}{\bar{a}}\Big)\bigg)

They suggest that a stronger initialization condition is necessary if
a_{1}-a_{2}
is smaller, i.e., the two edge probability matrices are closer. Furthermore, if
a_{1}-a_{2}\asymp\bar{a}
, the network sparsity condition (
11
) becomes
\bar{a}\to\infty
and the initialization requirements reduce to
\eta_{\sigma},\eta_{z}=o(1)
, namely, a consistent initial clustering suffices to guarantee the minimax optimal layer clustering.

2.5
Provable tensor-based initialization

The success of Algorithm
1
in achieving the minimax optimal clustering error relies on a warm initialization for both layer clustering and local community detection. We now consider a tensor-based spectral method, adapted from
Jing et al. 2021
, for an initial clustering.

To this end, some extra notations are necessary. Denote
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d\times n}
the
adjacency tensor
such that its
i
-th slice
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}(:,:,i):=\mathbf{X}_{i}
, and let
\mathbf{Z}_{1},\mathbf{Z}_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times K}
be the local membership matrix of two SBMs, respectively. Basically,
\mathbf{Z}_{m}(:,j)=\mathbf{e}^{\top}_{\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j)}
for
m\in[2]
where
\mathbf{e}_{j}
represent the
j
-th canonical basis vector whose dimension varies at different appearances. Under MMSBM, it is readily seen that
\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{X}_{i}|z_{i}^{\ast})=\mathbf{Z}_{z_{i}^{\ast}}\mathbf{B}_{z_{i}^{\ast}}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}_{z_{i}^{\ast}}
and the expected adjacency tensor is decomposable in the following format

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}|\mathbf{z}^{\ast})={\bm{\mathcal{B}}}\times_{1}\bar{}\mathbf{Z}\times_{2}\bar{}\mathbf{Z}\times_{3}\mathbf{W},

(13)

where
\bar{}\mathbf{Z}:=(\mathbf{Z}_{1},\mathbf{Z}_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{d\times 2K}
is called the
global membership
matrix,
\mathbf{W}:=(\mathbf{e}_{z_{1}^{\ast}},\mathbf{e}_{z_{2}^{\ast}}\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{z_{n}^{\ast}})^{\top}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times 2}
is the layer label matrix, and
{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}\in[0,1]^{2K\times 2K\times 2}
is a probability tensor with its
1
st and
2
nd slices are defined as

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}(:,:,1):=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{B}_{1}&0\\
0&0\\
\end{matrix}\right]\quad{\rm and}\quad{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}(:,:,2):=\left[\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&\mathbf{B}_{2}\\
\end{matrix}\right].

The multilinear product in (
13
) is defined in the way such that for any
i_{1},i_{2}\in[d]
and
i_{3}\in[n]
,
\mathbb{E}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})|\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{2K}\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{2K}\sum_{m\in\{1,2\}}{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}(j_{1},j_{2},m)\bar{}\mathbf{Z}(i_{1},j_{1})\bar{}\mathbf{Z}(i_{2},j_{2})\mathbf{W}(i_{3},m)
. For notational clarity, we use
\text{SVD}_{r}(\mathbf{M})
to denote the top
r
left singular vectors of any matrix
\mathbf{M}
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{k}({\bm{\mathcal{A}}})
to denote the mode-
k
matricization of any tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}
. See
Kolda and Bader 2009
for a more comprehensive introduction on tensor algebra.

Due to technical reasons, we focus on the regime
n,d\to\infty
and
n=O(d)
.
Denote
{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}=:\bar{p}{\bm{\mathcal{B}}}_{0}
with
\bar{p}:=\bar{a}(nd)^{-1}
. Without loss of generality, we focus on the most interesting yet challenging sparsity regime
\bar{a}\ll d
.
Let
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}:=\bar{}\mathbf{U}\bar{}\mathbf{D}\bar{}\mathbf{R}^{\top}
be the compact SVD with
\bar{}\mathbf{U},\bar{}\mathbf{R}
being left/right singular vectors of
\bar{}\mathbf{Z}
and
\bar{}\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
containing all non-zero singular values of
\bar{}\mathbf{Z}
, then the population adjacency tensor admits the
Tucker decomposition
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}|\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=\bar{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}\times_{1}\bar{}\mathbf{U}\times_{2}\bar{}\mathbf{U}\times_{3}\bar{}\mathbf{W},

(14)

where
\bar{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}={\bm{\mathcal{B}}}\times_{1}\bar{}\mathbf{D}\bar{}\mathbf{R}^{\top}\times_{2}\bar{}\mathbf{D}\bar{}\mathbf{R}^{\top}\times_{3}\mathbf{D}_{n}^{1/2}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r\times 2}
is a core tensor,
\mathbf{D}_{n}=\text{diag}(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
,
n_{m}=|{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z}^{\ast})|
is the number of layers in
m
-th clusters and
\bar{}\mathbf{W}:=\mathbf{W}\mathbf{D}_{n}^{-1/2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2}
. The matrices
\bar{}\mathbf{U}
and
\bar{}\mathbf{W}
are often referred to as the singular vectors of
\mathbb{E}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}|\mathbf{z}^{\ast})
, which provide the essential information of global community memberships and layer clusters.

The tensor-based spectral initialization algorithm proposed in
Jing et al. 2021
works as follows. The mode-
1
singular vectors
\bar{}\mathbf{U}
are estimated using the SVD of the sum of adjacency matrices
\sum_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i}
. This simple yet useful method is blessed by the non-negativity of the adjacency matrices. The empirical mode-
1
singular vectors are then truncated by the regularization operator defined as

{\cal P}_{\delta}(\mathbf{U}):=\text{SVD}_{r}(\mathbf{U}_{*})
with
\mathbf{U}_{*}(i,:):=\mathbf{U}(i,:)\cdot\min\{\delta,\left\|\mathbf{U}(i,:)\right\|\}/\left\|\mathbf{U}(i,:)\right\|
for any
\mathbf{U}
. The regularization guarantees the incoherence property in that
\left\|{\cal P}_{\delta}(\mathbf{U})\right\|_{2,\infty}=O(\delta)
. We assume that
\|\bar{}\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}=O\big((r/n)^{1/2}\big)
, i.e., the majority rows of
\bar{}\mathbf{U}
have comparable norms. Our theoretical results can be re-written to underscore the explicit dependence on
\|\bar{}\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}(n/r)^{1/2}
allowing unbounded incoherence parameter. For ease of exposition, we simply assume
\|\bar{}\mathbf{U}\|_{2,\infty}(n/r)^{1/2}=O(1)
.
The adjacency tensor is then multiplied by the regularized estimate of mode-
1
singular vectors, whose mode-
3
singular vectors, denoted by
\widehat{}\mathbf{W}
, are used as the estimate of
\bar{}\mathbf{W}
.
The layer clusters can be found by screening the rows of
\widehat{}\mathbf{W}
using, say,
K
-means clustering with
K=2
. Finally, the local community memberships are estimated by spectral clustering on aggregating networks with the same estimated layer labels. The detailed implementation can be found in Algorithm
2
.

Data:

adjacency tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d\times n}
, rank
r
, regularization parameters
\delta_{1}

Result:

layer clustering:
\tilde{}\mathbf{z}
and local community memberships
(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{1},\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{2})

1

Initializations of
\bar{}\mathbf{U}
and
\bar{}\mathbf{W}
:

2

\widehat{}\mathbf{U}=\text{SVD}_{r^{\ast}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i}\right)
,
\widehat{}\mathbf{W}=\text{SVD}_{2}\left({\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\times_{1}{\cal P}_{\delta_{1}}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{U}\right)\times_{2}{\cal P}_{\delta_{1}}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{U}\right)\right)\right)

3

Network clustering:
\tilde{}\mathbf{z}\longleftarrow
K-means clustering on rows of
\widehat{}\mathbf{W}

4

Node clustering:
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}\longleftarrow
K-means clustering on rows of
{\rm SVD}_{K}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}(\tilde{z}_{i}=m)\mathbf{X}_{i}\big),m\in[2]

5

Algorithm 2

Regularized spectral initial clustering (RSpec)

It has been shown by
Jing et al. 2021
that Algorithm
2
together with regularized tensor power iterations, referred to as TWIST, can provide a consistent layer clustering.
Unfortunately, the mis-clustering error rate established in
Jing et al. 2021
is rather weak such that an immediate application of their result will trivialize our conclusions in Theorem
2
. In particular,
Jing et al. 2021
showed that TWIST achieves the following layer mis-clustering error rate:

\displaystyle h(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{\textsf{\tiny TWIST}},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=O\Big(\frac{r^{2}\log(n\vee d)}{\bar{a}}\Big),

implying that a consistent clustering requires
\bar{a}\gg\log(n\vee d)
. The initialization condition by Theorem
2
requires
\eta_{z}\cdot\bar{a}K^{2}(1+dn^{-1})\ll I^{*}
means that

\displaystyle I^{*}\gg r^{2}K^{2}\Big(1+\frac{d}{n}\Big)\log(n\vee d)

(15)

However, in a regime of strong separation strength as in (
15
), Theorem
2
already implies that the two-stage clustering algorithm can achieve exact clustering since
h(\tilde{}\mathbf{z}^{\textsf{\tiny TWIST}},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})<1/n
is equivalent to
h(\tilde{}\mathbf{z}^{\textsf{\tiny TWIST}},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=0
.

We now present a simple variant of Algorithm
2
that enables us to eliminate the logarithmic term in (
15
). The basic idea is to introduce independence between
\widehat{}\mathbf{U}
and
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}
in Algorithm
2
so that a sharper perturbation bound of
\widehat{}\mathbf{W}
can be derived. In particular, we randomly split the network samples
[n]
and vertices
[d]
into two disjoint subsets of approximately equal size, denoted by
{\cal N}^{[0]}
,
{\cal N}^{[1]}
and
{\cal V}^{[0]},{\cal V}^{[1]}
, respectively. Here
{\cal N}^{[0]}\cup{\cal N}^{[1]}=[n]
,
{\cal V}^{[0]}\cup{\cal V}^{[1]}=[d]
, and assume
\widetilde{n}_{0}:=|{\cal N}^{[0]}|,\widetilde{n}_{1}=|{\cal N}^{[1]}|,d_{0}=|{\cal V}^{[0]}|,d_{1}=|{\cal V}^{[1]}|
. We focus on the sub-networks restricted to each subset of vertices. As a result, we end up with four multi-layer sub-networks, whose adjacency tensors are denoted as
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+}^{[0]}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d_{0}\times d_{0}\times\widetilde{n}_{0}}
,
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+}^{[1]}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}\times\widetilde{n}_{1}}
,
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{-}^{[0]}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}\times\widetilde{n}_{0}}
, and
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{-}^{[1]}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d_{0}\times d_{0}\times\widetilde{n}_{1}}
, respectively. The node and layer splitting is displayed as in Figure
1
. Without loss of generality, we assume that the node and layer splitting will not change the tensor ranks of
\mathbb{E}\big[{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+/-}^{[0/1]}|\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\big]
.

Figure 1
:
A diagram showing the node and layer sample splitting for initialization under MMSBM.

First, an estimate of mode-
1
singular vectors, denoted by
\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}
, is obtained utilizing
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[0]}_{+}
, similar as in Algorithm
2
. We then multiply
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[1]}_{+}
by the regularized version of
\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}
, from which we estimate the mode-
3
singular vectors and the layer labels of networks in
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[1]}_{+}
, denoted as
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]}
. Second, we switch the roles of
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[0]}_{+}
and
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[1]}_{+}
and estimate the layer labels of networks in
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[0]}
, denoted as
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}
, in the same fashion. The labels
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}
and
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]}
are both accurate estimates up to permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
. We align the labels of
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}
and
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]}
using reference layer labels estimated from the procedure without sample splitting. Finally, local community memberships can be estimated by spectral clustering applied onto the average adjacency matrices aggregated according to the estimated layer labels (also based on sample switching). The detailed implementations can be found in Algorithm
3
, where we choose
\delta_{1}=C_{0}(r/n)^{1/2}
with a tuning parameter
C_{0}>0
.

1

Data:

adjacency tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d\times n}
, rank
r
, number of communities
K
, regularization parameters
\delta_{1}
.

Result:

network labels
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}
, local memberships
\{\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{1},\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{2}\}
.

Reference labels
:
\left(\check{}\mathbf{z},\left\{\check{\bm{\sigma}}_{1},\check{\bm{\sigma}}_{2}\right\}\right)=\text{RSpec}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}},r,\delta_{1})

Sample splitting
: Let
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+}^{[0]}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{0}\times d_{0}\times\widetilde{n}_{0}}
,
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+}^{[1]}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}\times\widetilde{n}_{1}}
,
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{-}^{[0]}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}\times\widetilde{n}_{0}}
,
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{-}^{[1]}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{0}\times d_{0}\times\widetilde{n}_{1}}
(or
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}^{[+]}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{k}\times d_{k}}\}_{i\in{\cal N}^{[k]}}
and
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}^{[-]}\in\{0,1\}^{d_{k^{\prime}}\times d_{k^{\prime}}}\}_{i\in{\cal N}^{[k]}}
for
k\neq k^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
) be adjacency tensors (or samples) after sample splitting.

for

k\neq k^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}

do

Initializations of
\bar{}\mathbf{U}
and
\bar{}\mathbf{W}
:

\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[k^{\prime}]}=\text{SVD}_{r}\left(\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[k^{\prime}]}}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{[+]}\right)
,
\widehat{}\mathbf{W}^{[k]}=\text{SVD}_{2}\left({\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+}^{[k]}\times_{1}{\cal P}_{\delta_{1}}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[k^{\prime}]}\right)\times_{2}{\cal P}_{\delta_{1}}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[k^{\prime}]}\right)\right)\right)
.

\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[k]}=
K-means clustering on rows of
\widehat{}\mathbf{W}^{[k]}

Local memberships recovery:
for

m\in\{1,2\}

do

\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}^{[k^{\prime}]}
= K-means clustering on rows of
\text{SVD}_{K}\left(\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[k]}:\widetilde{z}_{i}^{[k]}=m}\mathbf{X}^{[-]}_{i}\right)

end for

end for

:
