end for

Algorithm 3

Tensor-based initialization with node and sample switching

Theorem 3
.

Suppose the following conditions (A1)-(A3) hold before and after the node and layer splitting:

(A1)

\bar{}\mathbf{B}_{0}
has Tucker rank
(r,r,2)
and
\sigma_{\min}(\bar{}\mathbf{B}_{0})\geq c
for some absolute constant
c>0
.

(A2)

\bar{}\mathbf{D}
is well-conditioned such that
\sigma_{1}(\bar{}\mathbf{D})/\sigma_{r}(\bar{}\mathbf{D})\leq\kappa_{0}
for some
\kappa_{0}\geq 1
.

(A3)

\sigma_{r}(n_{1}\mathbf{P}_{1}+n_{2}\mathbf{P}_{2})=\Omega(\sqrt{nd\bar{p}})
.

Furthermore, if
d\geq C_{0}\log^{3}(n\vee d)
and
n/\log n\geq C_{0}\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{6}
for some absolute constant
C_{0}>0
, then there exist some absolute constant
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4},C_{5}>0
such that, with probability at least
1-C_{1}(n\vee d)^{-3}
, the output of Algorithm
1
satisfies

h(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\frac{C_{2}\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{2}}{\bar{a}}\quad{\rm and}\quad\max_{m\in\{1,2\}}h(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m},\bm{\sigma}_{m})\leq\frac{C_{3}}{\bar{a}},

provided that
C_{4}\kappa_{0}^{8}r^{4}\log^{2}(\kappa_{0}r)\log^{7}(n\vee d)\leq\bar{a}\leq C_{5}\left(n\wedge d\right)/\log^{2}n
.

The conditions (A1)-(A3) were originally proposed by
Jing et al. 2021
in the context of MMSBM. Due to the rank-deficiency of
\bar{}\mathbf{Z}
, conditions (A1)-(A2) emerge as the prices of exploiting tensor structures, as discussed in details in
Jing et al. 2021
.
Moreover, condition (A3) is a standard condition, probably unavoidable for spectral method by aggregating multiple adjacency matrices. We note that our condition (A3) is weaker compared with the counterparts required in Lemma 5.6 in
Jing et al. 2021
and Theorem 2 in
Paul and Chen 2020b
, in terms of logarithmic factor. In particular, our condition (A3) allows a diverging condition number of
n_{1}^{\ast}\mathbf{P}_{1}+n_{2}^{\ast}\mathbf{P}_{2}
.

Consider the extreme sparse case (
9
) in that
p_{m}dn=a_{m}
and
q_{m}dn=b_{m}
. Let
\kappa_{0},K,r=O(1)
for simplicity.
Theorem
3
and Theorem
2
together require

\displaystyle\text{(i)}\quad\min_{m\in\{1,2\}}\left(a_{m}-b_{m}\right)=\Omega(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\text{(ii)}\quad I^{*}\gg K^{2}\left(\frac{d}{n}+1\right)\sqrt{\log(\bar{a}\wedge d)}

Condition (i) is mild compared to the condition for achieving exponential rate in the community detection in a single network
Gao et al. 2017
, which requires
(a_{m}-b_{m})^{2}/a_{m}\rightarrow\infty
. Ours is weaker because we do not seek an optimal clustering of vertices.
For dense networks where
\bar{a}>C_{3}d
, local memberships can be exactly recovered by Algorithm
3
and hence
\eta_{\sigma}\log\frac{1}{\eta_{\sigma}}=0
. Thus condition (ii) is satisfied if
I^{*}\gg K^{2}\left(d/n+1\right)\log^{1/2}d
, which is much weaker than (
15
) when
d=o\left(n\right)
. Even when
n\lesssim d\leq n^{m}
for any constant
m>1
, condition (ii) gains a non-trivial improvement from
\log(n)
to
\log^{1/2}n
comparing to (
16
).

The most interesting case is when
\bar{a}\asymp\log^{5}(n\vee d)
(the most difficult regime in that the networks are extremely sparse), condition (ii) is fulfilled by

\displaystyle I^{*}\gg K^{2}\left(\frac{d}{n}+1\right)\sqrt{\log\log(n\vee d)},

which is slightly stronger than condition (
16
) in Theorem
2
by a factor of
\log\log(n\vee d)
. The following corollary is a formal statement of aforementioned argument.

Corollary 1
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3
, assume that there exists absolute constant
c_{0},C_{0}>0
such that
\log n\leq C_{0}d
and
\min_{m\in\{1,2\}}\left(a_{m}-b_{m}\right)\geq c_{0}K
.
There exist absolute constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
such that if
\underline{a}\geq C

\displaystyle\frac{I^{*}}{K^{2}\left(\frac{d}{n}+1\right)\sqrt{\log(\bar{a}\wedge d)}}\rightarrow\infty,

(16)

then with probability
1-\exp(-I^{\ast(1-\epsilon)})
for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,

h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\Big(-(1-o(1))\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\Big),

and

\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\Big(-(1-o(1))\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\Big)+O\left(n^{-C^{\prime}}\right).

3
Optimal Clustering of Mixture Multi-layer Poisson Networks

3.1
Mixture of Poisson Block Models

Count-type data arise in many scientific and econometric applications such as, 4D-STEM imaging
(
Han et al. 2022
)
, cell counts in spatial-transcriptome study
(
Moses and Pachter 2022
)
, and trade flow networks
(
Lyu et al. 2021
;
Cai et al. 2022
)
. A Poisson block model (PBM) can be viewed as a generalization of SBM to count-type data. Without loss of generality, we view PBM as a model for weighted networks. A weighted network
{\cal G}
is observed with
d
vertices, each of which belongs to one of
K
communities. Let
\bm{\sigma}:[d]\mapsto[K]
denote the community assignment defined similarly as in SBM. There is a symmetric matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K\times K}
carrying the Poisson weight. The network
{\cal G}
carries random weight, or equivalently, the entries of its adjacency matrix
\mathbf{X}
, generated from

\mathbf{X}(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ind.}}}{{\sim}}\ {\rm Poisson}\big(\mathbf{B}\big(\bm{\sigma}(\omega)\big)\big),\quad\forall\ \omega\in{\cal J}_{d}:=\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq j\leq d\}

which is referred to as the PBM
(\bm{\sigma},\mathbf{B})
.

Suppose we observe a collection of weighted networks
\{\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of common
d
nodes, i.e., a multi-layer weighted network. Each network is either sampled from PBM
(\bm{\sigma}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{1})
or PBM
(\bm{\sigma}_{2},\mathbf{B}_{2})
. Denote
\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\in[2]^{n}
the layer label vector meaning that
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\sim{\rm PBM}(\bm{\sigma}_{z_{i}^{\ast}},\mathbf{B}_{z_{i}^{\ast}})
. Let
\mathbf{X}_{i}
denote the adjacency matrix of
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{i}
whose entries follow Poisson distributions as

\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ind.}}}{{\sim}}\ {\rm Poisson}\big(\mathbf{B}_{z^{\ast}_{i}}(\bm{\sigma}_{z_{i}^{\ast}}(\omega))\big),\quad\forall i\in[n].

(17)

We call it the mixture multi-layer Poisson block model (MMPBM), which models both layer clusters and nodal communities in multi-layer weighted networks.
After observing the networks
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{i},i\in[n]
, our goal is to find their latent labels. The notations
{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z}^{\ast}),S_{k}(\bm{\sigma}_{m})
, and
n_{1}^{\ast},n_{2}^{\ast}
are defined similarly as in Section
2
.

Throughout this section, we focus on the following parameter space of MMPBM:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Theta}:=\mathbf{\Theta}(n,d,K,\theta_{0},\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\bigg\{\left(\mathbf{z},\{\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2}\},\{\bm{\sigma}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{2}\}\right):\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n},|{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z})|\in\left[\frac{n}{2\alpha},\frac{\alpha n}{2}\right],

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}_{m}:[d]\rightarrow[K],|{\cal S}_{k}(\bm{\sigma}_{m})|\in\left[\frac{d}{\beta K},\frac{\beta d}{K}\right],\mathbf{B}_{m}=\mathbf{B}_{m}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K\times K},

\displaystyle\min_{m\in[1,2]}\min_{i,j}\mathbf{B}_{m}(i,j)\geq\theta_{0}\quad{\rm and}\quad\max_{m\in[1,2]}\max_{i,j}\mathbf{B}_{m}(i,j)\leq\gamma\theta_{0}\bigg\},

(18)

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\geq 1
are assumed to be absolute constants and
\theta_{0}>0
is a constant characterizing the overall edge intensity. The condition that
\theta_{0}
is bounded away from zero is mild. See,
Han et al. 2022
and
Cai et al. 2022
.
Denote
\mathbf{P}_{m}(j_{1},j_{2}):=\mathbf{B}_{m}\left(\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j_{1}),\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j_{2})\right)
the edge intensity matrix, i.e., the expectation of adjacency matrix under PBM
\left(\mathbf{B}_{m},\bm{\sigma}_{m}\right)
.

3.2
Oracle property of likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm

Suppose that the edge intensity matrices
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
are known in advance, if an observed network
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d\times d}
is known sampled from either
\mathbf{P}_{1}
or
\mathbf{P}_{2}
, the log-likelihood can be written as

\ell(\mathbf{X}|z):=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bigg(-\mathbf{P}_{z}(\omega)+\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\mathbf{P}_{z}(\omega)-\log\big(\mathbf{X}(\omega)!\big)\bigg),

where
z
takes values in
[2]
. The likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm assigns the label to
\mathbf{X}
which maximizes the log-likelihood
\ell(\mathbf{X}|z)
. Without loss of generality, assume the true label of
\mathbf{X}
is
1
. This network is mis-clustered by Lloyd’s algorithm if

\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\big(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\big)>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}

The following lemma characterizes the oracle mis-clustering error rate of Lloyd’s algorithm.

Lemma 3
.

Suppose that the edge probability matrices
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
are known, and a new observed network
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from the probability matrix
\mathbf{P}_{1}
., i.e.,
\mathbf{X}(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ind.}}}{{\sim}}{\rm Poisson}(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))
for
\forall\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}
. The probability of mis-clustering
\mathbf{X}
by Lloyd’s algorithm is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{X}\textrm{ is mis-clustered})\leq\exp\left(-I^{*}/2\right),

where
I^{*}=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\big(\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}-\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\big)^{2}
. Similarly, the same bound holds if
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from the probability matrix
\mathbf{P}_{2}
.

Note that the Rényi-divergence of order
1/2
of
{\rm Poisson}\big(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\big)
from
{\rm Poisson}\big(\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\big)
is defined by
\big(\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}-\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\big)^{2}
. The quantity
I^{\ast}
is thus viewed as the Rényi-divergence between the two joint Poisson distributions. Hereafter,
I^{\ast}
is regarded as the SNR under MMPBM. It shows that a necessary condition of consistently clustering multiple Poisson networks is
I^{\ast}\to\infty
. We remark that Poisson is sub-exponential, while the distributions under MMSBM (Section
2
) and LrMM
(
Lyu and Xia 2022
)
are sub-Gaussian.

3.3
Minimax lower bound

We now characterize the minimax lower bound of clustering networks under MMPBM. Similarly, we fix the node community memberships
\bm{\sigma}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{2}
and the edge intensity matrices
\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2}
. Denote
\mathbf{B}_{1,2}:=\{\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2}\}
,
\bm{\sigma}_{1,2}:=\{\bm{\sigma}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{2}\}
, and

\mathbf{\Theta}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\ \bm{\sigma}_{1,2})}:=\big\{\mathbf{z}:\ \big(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2}\big)\textrm{ satisfies constraints in }\eqref{eq:parameter-space-MMPBM}\big\}

The minimax lower bound in the following theorem presents an exponential rate, matching the oracle rate in Lemma
3
.

Theorem 4
.

For fixed
\mathbf{B}_{1,2}
and
\bm{\sigma}_{1,2}
satisfying the constraints in (
3.1
), let
\mathbf{P}_{1}=\mathbf{B}_{1}\circ\bm{\sigma}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}=\mathbf{B}_{2}\circ\bm{\sigma}_{2}
, and define

I^{\ast}:=I^{\ast}(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\big(\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}-\sqrt{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\big)^{2}

If
I^{\ast}\to\infty
, then

\inf_{\widehat{}\mathbf{z}}\sup_{\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\in\mathbf{\Theta}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})}}\ \mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\geq\exp\Big(-\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\cdot\big(1+o(1)\big)\Big),

where the infimum is taken over all possible clustering algorithms working on MMPBM with parameters
(\mathbf{z}^{\ast},\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})
.

3.4
Two-stage clustering

By Lemma
3
and Theorem
4
, the likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm achieves optimal clustering error if the edge intensity matrix
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
are known. Since they must be estimated, we first study the necessary accuracy of estimated edge intensity matrices that ensures the optimality of likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm. It turns out that if the estimated edge intensity matrices are accurate with the
\ell_{1}
-norm error bounded by
o(I^{\ast})
, the likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm still achieves optimal clustering error.

Lemma 4
.

Suppose that a network
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from the edge intensity matrix
\mathbf{P}_{1}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{X}(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ind.}}}{{\sim}}{\rm Poisson}(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))
for
\forall\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}
. Let
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}
be the edge probability matrices estimated without using
\mathbf{X}
. Let
\widehat{z}
be the MLE of
\mathbf{X}
’s label based on
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}
:

\widehat{z}:=\underset{z\in\{1,2\}}{\arg\max}\ \sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bigg(-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{z}(\omega)+\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{z}(\omega)\big)\bigg)

(19)

If
\|\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}-\mathbf{P}_{1}\|_{\ell_{1}},\|\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}-\mathbf{P}_{2}\|_{\ell_{1}}=o(I^{\ast})
where
I^{\ast}
is as defined in Lemma
3
and
I^{\ast}\to\infty
, then, conditioned on
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{z}=2)\leq\exp\bigg(-\big(1-o(1)\big)\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\bigg)

The same bound still holds if
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from the probability matrix
\mathbf{P}_{2}
.

Based on Lemma
4
, we develop a two-stage algorithm for clustering networks under MMPBM. The algorithm starts with an initial estimate of the layer labels and node community memberships. Following the same ideas as Algorithm
1
, we then estimate the edge intensity matrices and use them for estimating layer labels by the likelihood-based Lloyd’s algorithm. For self-completeness, the detailed implementations are provided in Algorithm
4
. Suppose that the initialization algorithm ensures

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big\{\max_{i\in[n]}h(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}^{(-i)},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\eta_{z}\Big\}\ \bigcap\Big\{\max_{i\in[n];i\in[2]}h(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}^{(-i)},\bm{\sigma}_{m})\leq\eta_{\sigma}\Big\}\Big)\geq 1-C_{1}(n\vee d)^{-2}

(20)

for some absolute constant
C_{1}>0
. Here
\eta_{z},\eta_{\sigma}\to 0
as
n,d\to\infty
and they reflect the effectiveness of the initialization algorithm.

Data:

network samples
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
K
-the number of communities in each network,
\text{Init}(\cdot)
-an initialization algorithm for estimating network labels and local community memberships.

Result:

network label
\widehat{}\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n}

Run Algorithm
3
but replace (
7
) with

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}=\argmax_{m\in[2]}\Bigg[

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bigg(-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega)+\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega)\log\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}^{(-i)}(\omega)\big)\bigg)\Bigg]

(21)

1

Algorithm 4

Two-stage network clustering under MMPBM

Theorem 5
.

Suppose that the initialization algorithm satisfies (
20
) with,

\eta_{\sigma}\log\frac{1}{\eta_{\sigma}}=o\bigg(\frac{I^{*2}}{K^{2}d^{2}\theta_{0}(d/n)}\bigg)\quad{\rm and}\quad\eta_{\sigma}\vee\eta_{z}=o\bigg(\frac{I^{\ast}}{K^{2}d^{2}\theta_{0}}\bigg)

(22)

If
\underline{a}\geq C_{0}
for some absolute constants
C_{0}
,
K^{2}\log n=o(n)
, and
I^{\ast}\to\infty
, then, conditioned on the event (
20
), with probability at least
1-\exp\left(-(I^{*})^{1-\epsilon}\right)
for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, the output of Algorithm
4
satisfies

h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\Big(-(1-o(1))\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\Big).

Moreover,

\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\Big(-(1-o(1))\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\Big)+O\left(n^{-C^{\prime}}\right),

and some large constant
C^{\prime}>0
.

4
Mixture of Discrete Distributions

4.1
Mixture of Binomial

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
be sampled i.i.d. from a mixture of Binomial distributions:
\frac{1}{2}{\rm Bin}(d,p_{1})+\frac{1}{2}{\rm Bin}(d,p_{2})
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
n_{1}
of them are sampled from
{\rm Bin}(d,p_{1})
and
n_{2}
sampled from
{\rm Bin}(d,p_{2})
, where
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and
n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
. Denote
\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\in[2]^{n}
encoding the latent labels, i.e.,
X_{i}\sim{\rm Bin}(d,p_{z^{\ast}_{i}})
, which is assumed to be fixed. We focus on the difficult regime where
p_{1}\asymp p_{2}
,
d^{-1}\ll p=o(1)
, and
d
is known.
Let

\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})^{\top}\quad{\rm and}\quad\mathbf{m}^{\ast}:=(dp_{z^{\ast}_{1}},\cdots,dp_{z^{\ast}_{n}})^{\top}=\mathbb{E}\mathbf{X}

Note that if
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are known, the maximum likelihood estimator of
X
’s label is defined by

\ell_{\textsf{\tiny ML}}(X):=\mathbb{I}\Big(X\log\frac{p_{2}(1-p_{1})}{p_{1}(1-p_{2})}\geq d\log\frac{1-p_{1}}{1-p_{2}}\Big)+1

It suffices to have an accurate estimate of the probabilities
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Here we apply a similar leave-one-out trick as in Algorithm
1
to decouple the dependence between estimated probabilities and the samples to be clustered. Denote
\mathbf{X}^{(-i)}
the
(n-1)
-dimensional sub-vector of
\mathbf{X}
by deleting its
i
-th entry and
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}\in[2]^{n}
an outcome of clustering the entries of
\mathbf{X}^{(-i)}
, where we set
\widetilde{z}_{i}^{(-i)}=0
for ease of exposition. Similarly, define
\widetilde{p}_{1}^{(-i)}
and
\widetilde{p}_{2}^{(-i)}
the estimated Binomial probabilities based on
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}
, i.e.,

\widetilde{p}_{k}^{(-i)}:=\frac{\sum_{j\in[n]}X_{j}\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=k)}{d|\{j\in[n]:\widetilde{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=k\}|},\quad\forall k\in[2].

The detailed implementations can be found in Algorithm
5
. Here we consider two initialization methods: K-means and method of moments (MoM).

Data:

\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})^{\top}
,
d

Result:

labels
\widehat{}\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n}

for

i\in[n]

do

1

Initialization:
(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)},\widetilde{p}_{1}^{(-i)},\widetilde{p}_{2}^{(-i)})=\textsf{K-means}\big(\mathbf{X}^{(-i)}\big)
or
(\widetilde{p}_{1}^{(-i)},\widetilde{p}_{2}^{(-i)})=\textsf{MoM}\big(\mathbf{X}^{(-i)}\big)

2

Let
\widehat{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=\widetilde{z}_{j}^{(-i)}
for all
j\neq i\in[n]
and

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}=\argmax_{k\in[2]}\Big[X_{i}\log\widetilde{p}_{k}^{(-i)}+(d-X_{i})\log\big(1-\widetilde{p}_{k}^{(-i)}\big)\Big]

(23)

end for

3

Let
\widehat{z}_{1}=\widehat{z}_{1}^{(-1)}

4

for

i=2

to

n

do

5

\widehat{z}_{i}=\argmax_{k\in\{1,2\}}\left|\left\{j\in[n]:\widehat{z}_{j}^{(-1)}=k\right\}\bigcap\left\{j\in[n]:\widehat{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}\right\}\right|

end for

6

Algorithm 5

Optimal Clustering of Mixture of Binomials

The MoM was first proposed by
Blischke 1964
for estimating the probability parameters in the mixture of Binomial distributions. Let

\widehat{M}_{1}:=\frac{1}{nd}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\quad{\rm and}\quad\widehat{M}_{2}:=\frac{1}{nd(d-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}^{2}-X_{i}).

It is easy to check that
2\mathbb{E}\widehat{M}_{1}=p_{1}+p_{2}
and
2\mathbb{E}\widehat{M}_{2}=p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}
. Without loss of generality, assume
p_{1}\geq p_{2}
. The MoM estimates are
\widehat{p}_{1}:=\widehat{M}_{1}+(\widehat{M}_{2}-\widehat{M}_{1}^{2})^{1/2}
and
\widehat{p}_{2}:=\widehat{M}_{1}-(\widehat{M}_{2}-\widehat{M}_{1}^{2})^{1/2}
.

The performance of Algorithm
5
hinges upon the accuracy of initializations. Denote the event

{\cal E}_{0n}:=\bigg\{\max\nolimits_{i\in[n]}\big|\widetilde{p}_{1}^{(-i)}-p_{1}\big|+\big|\widetilde{p}_{2}^{(-i)}-p_{2}\big|\leq\frac{c_{n}I^{\ast}}{d}\bigg\},

(24)

where
c_{n}\to 0
is an arbitrary positive sequence. Here
I^{\ast}:=-2d\log\big((1-p_{1})^{1/2}(1-p_{2})^{1/2}+(p_{1}p_{2})^{1/2}\big)
is the Rényi-
1/2
divergence between
{\rm Bin}(d,p_{1})
and
{\rm Bin}(d,p_{2})
.

Theorem 6
.

If
I^{\ast}\to\infty
, then under
{\cal E}_{0n}
, the output of Algorithm
5
achieves the error rate

\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=\exp\bigg\{-\big(1-o(1)\big)\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\bigg\},

and, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\exp\big(-(I^{\ast}/2)^{1-\varepsilon}\big)
,

h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=\exp\bigg\{-\big(1-o(1)\big)\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\bigg\}

We remark that if
p_{1},p_{2}=o(1)
, then
I^{\ast}\to\infty
implies that
d(p_{1}-p_{1})\gg 1
.
A minimax lower bound of the form
\exp(-I^{\ast}/2)
can established similarly as in Theorem
1
. Theorem
6
shows that Algorithm
5
can achieve minimax optimal clustering error rate if well initialized. Now it suffices to investigate the performance of the K-means initialization and the method of moments.

Lemma 5
.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that for any
\gamma>2
if

d(p_{1}-p_{2})^{2}\geq C\gamma p_{1},\quad nd(p_{1}-p_{2})^{2}\geq C\gamma,\quad{\rm and}\quad I^{\ast}\gg C\gamma\Big(\frac{p_{1}}{p_{1}-p_{2}}\Big)^{2}\cdot\Big(1+\frac{1}{np_{1}}\Big),

then, with probability at least
1-10^{-\gamma}
, the K-means initial clustering outputs

\max_{i\in[n]}\big|\widetilde{p}_{1}^{(-i)}-p_{1}\big|+\big|\widetilde{p}_{2}^{(-i)}-p_{2}\big|=o\Big(\frac{I^{\ast}}{d}\Big).

(25)

On the other hand, the method of moments guarantees (
25
) with probability at least
1-14n^{-2}
if

I^{\ast}\gg 1+\frac{p_{1}}{|p_{1}-p_{2}|}\sqrt{\frac{dp_{1}(dp_{1}+\log n)\log n}{n}}

4.2
Mixture of Poisson

We now consider the clustering of count-type data. More specifically, let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
be sampled i.i.d. from a mixture of Poisson distributions:
\frac{1}{2}{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{1})+\frac{1}{2}{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{2})
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
n_{1}
of them are sampled from
{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{1})
and
n_{2}
sampled from
{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{2})
, where
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and
n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
. Let
\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\in[2]^{n}
encode the latent labels, i.e.,
X_{i}\sim{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{z^{\ast}_{i}})
, which is assumed to be fixed. Similarly, we focus on the difficult regime
\theta_{1}\asymp\theta_{2}
and
|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|^{2}\gg\theta_{1}\gg 1
.
Let

\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})^{\top}\quad{\rm and}\quad\mathbf{m}^{\ast}:=(\theta_{z^{\ast}_{1}},\cdots,\theta_{z^{\ast}_{n}})^{\top}=\mathbb{E}\mathbf{X}

The maximum likelihood estimator of
X
’s label given
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
is defined by

\ell_{\textsf{\tiny ML}}(X):=\mathbb{I}\Big(X\log\frac{\theta_{2}}{\theta_{1}}\geq\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\Big)+1

It suffices to have an accurate estimate of the probabilities
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
. Define
\mathbf{X}^{(-i)}
and
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}
as in Section
4.1
. The the estimated Poisson probabilities
\widetilde{\theta}_{1}^{(-i)}
and
\widetilde{\theta}_{2}^{(-i)}
based on
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}
are defined by

\widetilde{\theta}_{k}^{(-i)}:=\frac{\sum_{j\in[n]}X_{j}\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=k)}{|\{j\in[n]:\widetilde{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=k\}|},\quad\forall k\in[2].

The detailed implementations can be found in Algorithm
6
. Here the MoM works as follows. Let

\widehat{M}_{1}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\quad{\rm and}\quad\widehat{M}_{\tiny 1/2}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{X_{i}}.

Clearly, their expectations
M_{1}=\mathbb{E}\widehat{M}_{1}=(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})/2
and
M_{\tiny 1/2}=\mathbb{E}\widehat{M}_{\tiny 1/2}=(\theta_{1}^{1/2}+\theta_{2}^{1/2})/2+O(\theta_{1}^{-1/2})
. Assume that
\theta_{1}>\theta_{2}
, then the MoM estimators are

\widehat{\theta}_{1}:=\widehat{M}_{1}+2\widehat{M}_{\tiny 1/2}\sqrt{\widehat{M}_{1}-\widehat{M}_{\tiny 1/2}^{2}}\quad{\rm and}\quad\widehat{\theta}_{2}:=\widehat{M}_{1}-2\widehat{M}_{\tiny 1/2}\sqrt{\widehat{M}_{1}-\widehat{M}_{\tiny 1/2}^{2}}.

The proof of the following theorem is almost identical to that of Theorem
6
and is thus skipped.

Data:

\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})^{\top}

Result:

labels
\widehat{}\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n}

Run Algorithm
5
but replace (
23
) by

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}=\argmax_{k\in[2]}\Big[-\widetilde{\theta}_{k}^{(-i)}+X_{i}\log\widetilde{\theta}_{k}^{(-i)}\Big]

(26)

Algorithm 6

Optimal Clustering of Mixture of Poissons

Denote the event

{\cal E}_{0n}:=\Big\{\max\nolimits_{i\in[n]}\big|\widetilde{\theta}_{1}^{(-i)}-\theta_{1}\big|+\big|\widetilde{\theta}_{2}^{(-i)}-\theta_{2}\big|\leq c_{n}I^{\ast}\Big\},

(27)

where
c_{n}\to 0
is an arbitrary positive sequence. Here
I^{\ast}:=\big(\sqrt{\theta_{1}}-\sqrt{\theta_{2}}\big)^{2}
is the Rényi-
1/2
divergence between
{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{1})
and
{\rm Poisson}(\theta_{2})
.

Theorem 7
.

If
I^{\ast}\to\infty
, then under
{\cal E}_{0n}
, the output of Algorithm
6
achieves the error rate

\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=\exp\bigg\{-\big(1-o(1)\big)\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\bigg\},

and, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\exp\big(-(I^{\ast}/2)^{1-\varepsilon}\big)
,

h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=\exp\bigg\{-\big(1-o(1)\big)\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\bigg\}

Note that
I^{\ast}\to\infty
implies that
(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}\gg\theta_{1}
, which implies that
|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|\gg 1
.
The theoretical guarantees of the K-means initialization or the MoM initialization can be similarly established as in the case of Binomial mixtures.

Lemma 6
.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that for any
\gamma>2
if

(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}\geq C{\gamma}\theta_{1},\quad n(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}\geq C{\gamma},\quad{\rm and}\quad I^{\ast}\gg C{\gamma}\frac{\theta_{1}}{(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}}\cdot\Big(1+\frac{1}{n\theta_{1}}\Big),

then, with probability at least
1-10^{-\gamma}
, the K-means initial clustering outputs

\max_{i\in[n]}\big|\widetilde{\theta}_{1}^{(-i)}-\theta_{1}\big|+\big|\widetilde{\theta}_{2}^{(-i)}-\theta_{2}\big|=o\Big(I^{\ast}\Big).

(28)

On the other hand, the method of moments guarantees (
28
) with probability at least
1-2n^{-2}
if

I^{\ast}\gg\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}+\bigg(\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}}{\big(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\big)^{2}}+\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{\sqrt{\theta_{1}}}\bigg)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\theta_{1}\log n+\log^{2}n}{n}}

and
n\geq C\log^{2}n
.

5.1
Simulation studies

We conduct several simulations to test the performance of the refinement algorithm on the MMSBM with different choices of network sparsity, “out-in” ratio, number of layers and the size of each layer. We use K-means as the clustering algorithm.
We compare the mis-clustering rate across 150 replications of each experiment to identify the average performance.

We generate the data according to MMSBM in the following fashion. The underlying class
s_{l}
for the
l
-th layer is generated from the multinomial distribution with
\mathbb{P}(s_{l}=j)=1/2,\ j=1,2.
The membership
z_{i}^{j}
for node
i
in layer type
j
is generated from the multinomial distribution with
\mathbb{P}(z_{i}^{j}=s)=1/K,\ s=1,\cdots,K.
We choose the probability matrix as

B=pI_{K}+q(1_{K}1_{K}^{\top}-I_{K}),
where
1_{K}
is a
K
-dimensional all-one vector and
I_{K}
is the
K\times K
identity matrix . Let
\alpha=q/p
be the out-in ratio.

We compare the performance of the refinement algorithm with the TWIST
(
Jing et al. 2021
)
, Tucker decomposition initialized by HOSVD (HOSVD-Tucker) and spectral clustering applied to the mode-3 flatting of
\mathbf{A}
(M3-SC). The function “
tucker
” from the R package ”
rTensor
”
Li et al. 2018
is used to apply Tucker decomposition for HOSVD-Tucker.

In Simulation 1, the networks are generated with the number of nodes
n=40
, the number of layers
L=40
, number of types of networks
m=2,
number of communities of each network
K=2
and out-in ratio of each layer
\alpha=0.75.
The
p
of each layer varies from
0.1
to
0.8
, which reflects the average degree with fixed out-in ratio.

In Simulation 2, the networks are generated as in Simulation 1, except that the average degree of each layer
p=0.4,
the number of layers
L=40
and the out-in ration
\alpha
of each layer varies from
0.1
to
0.9.

In Simulation 3, the networks are the same as in Simulation 2, except that the out-in ratio
\alpha=0.75
and the number of layers
L
varies from
20
to
80.

In Simulation 4, the networks are the same as in Simulation 3, except that the average degree of each layer
L=40
and the the size of each layer
n
varies from
10
to
100.

n=40
,

,

,

, varying

,

Figure 2
:
The refinement algorithm is the best overall, particularly when the signal is not strong enough, for instance,
p<0.5
in (a),
\alpha>0.6
in (b), all the scenario in (c), and
n<60
in (d). From Simulations 3, other there methods reach the limit to 95% accuracy rate, when
L
increases to 80. However, the refinement algorithm nearly achieves 100% accuracy when
L
is only 40.

The results of the simulations are shown in Figure
2
, from which we can draw several key findings:

1.

As we anticipated from our theoretical results, mis-clustering rates for all three methods decrease when the average degree of each layer increases, the out-in ratio of each layer decreases, the number of layers increases, and the size of each layer increases.

2.

Our refinement algorithm notably surpasses the other methods in terms of accuracy as the number of layers increases. While the other methods only achieve a 95% accuracy rate when the number of layers reaches 80, the refinement algorithm nearly hits 100% accuracy with only 40 layers (as observed in Simulation 3).

3.

The refinement algorithm consistently outperforms the other three methods across all simulations. Its superior performance is especially noticeable when the signal strength is relatively weak, for example when
p<0.5
in Simulation 1,
\alpha>0.6
in Simulation 2, in all scenarios in Simulation 3, and when
n<60
in Simulation 4.

4.

While the refinement algorithm uses TWIST as an initialization step, the improvements obtained from the refinement step are significant, indicating its pivotal role in the performance of the algorithm.

5.2
Worldwide food trading networks

For real application, we consider the dataset on the worldwide food trading networks, which is collected by
De Domenico et al. 2015
and have been widely adopted in muli-layer network analysis
(
Jing et al. 2021
;
Fan et al. 2021
)
. The data contains an economic network in which layers represent different products, nodes are countries and edges at each layer represent trading relationships of a specific food product among countries.

We focus on the trading data in 2010 only. Following the data prepossess in
Jing et al. 2021
, we obtained a 30-layers network with 99 nodes at each layer. Each layer represents trading relationships between 99 countries/regions worldwide with respect to one of the 30 different food products. Together they form a
3
rd-order tensor of dimension
99\times 99\times 30
.

We apply the refinement algorithm to the data tensor. The resulting two clusters of layers are listed in Table
1
. We then apply the spectral method to the sum of each cluster of networks separately (here we have two clusters) to find the community structures for each cluster, in order to obtain the clustering result of countries. The membership of 99 countries with clustering results from 4-means are shown in Figure
3
.
For the two types of networks, we plot in Figure
4
the sum of adjacency matrices with nodes arranged according to the community labels to have a glance of different community structures of two network types.

We make the following remarks from Table
1
, Figures
3
and
4
.

1.

Food types are mainly divided into two categories. The first category comprises food products that have a longer shelf life, facilitating a more globalized trading pattern. Conversely, the second category includes foods that typically have a shorter shelf life or whose transportation costs are high relative to their overall value. Thus, the trade of these foods is predominantly conducted within nearby regions.

2.

From the first category, a handful of countries, such as China, Canada, the United Kingdom, the United States, France, and Germany, are notably active in trading both internationally and amongst themselves. However, outside of these hub nations, other countries are grouped mainly by geographical location, such as America, East Europe, Africa, and Australia. This pattern demonstrates the dominance of these hub countries in the global food trade, while other nations engage more in regional trading to minimize delivery costs.

3.

In the second category, countries are primarily clustered based on their geographical location. Apart from the USA, which is grouped with European countries, the four main clusters consist of America, Western Europe, Eastern Europe and West Africa, and nations surrounding the Indian Ocean - Asia, East Africa, and Australia. Regional trading is crucial in this category as it helps to keep food costs low due to reduced transportation costs and maintains food freshness due to faster delivery times.

5.3
Brain connectivity networks

We test our proposed method using the COBRE dataset (
Aine et al. 2017
;
Paul and Chen 2020a
), which consists of resting state fMRI experiments conducted on both diagnosed schizophrenia patients and healthy control subjects. The brain network is defined as the connectivity among various regions of a person’s brain. The focus of this study is on functional connectivity - a statistical measure that denotes the correlation between each pair of locations in the brain, obtained from functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) data. Given the label,
Paul and Chen 2020a
took a deep dive into understanding the differences in the community structure of the Region of Interest (ROI) between schizophrenia patients and control participants’ connectivity networks.

The processed connectomic dataset used is downloaded from
Relión et al. 2019
. It includes connectivity networks of 54 schizophrenia patients and 70 healthy controls, each represented as a
263\times 263
matrix. Weak connections in the network matrix are transmuted to 0 by taking the median of the mean network constructed from all samples as the threshold. All other strong connections are converted to 1.

Results of all methods used within the simulation study are reported, comparing misclustering errors with their true labels - schizophrenia patients or healthy controls - as shown in Table
2
. The proposed method showed an accuracy rate of
60.5\%
, superseding other methods tested. It is worth to note that while an higher accuracy rate was reported in
Relión et al. 2019
, their model was based on supervised learning. Conversely, the task in our study is unsupervised learning.

Refinement

Tucker

M3-SC

Error

0.444

0.435

0.492

:
Clustering error of COBRE data.

6
Discussions

7
Proof of Main Results

7.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Recall that
{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z})=\{i\in[n]:z_{i}=m\}
for a label vector
\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n}
and
m\in[2]
. With slight abuse of notation, let
\mathbf{\Theta}_{z}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n}:|{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z})|\in\left[\frac{n}{2\alpha},\frac{\alpha n}{2}\right]\}
. For any fixed
\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathbf{\Theta}_{z}
with
n_{m}^{*}=|{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z}^{*})|
, we can choose any subset
{\mathfrak{N}}_{m}\subset{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z}^{*})
with cardinality
|{\mathfrak{N}}_{m}|=n_{m}^{*}-\lfloor\frac{n}{8}\rfloor
. Let
{\mathfrak{N}}:={\mathfrak{N}}_{1}\bigcup{\mathfrak{N}}_{2}
and define

\mathbf{Z}_{{\mathfrak{N}}}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n}:z_{i}=z_{i}^{*},\forall i\in{\mathfrak{N}}\}

Note that
{\mathfrak{N}}_{1}\bigcap{\mathfrak{N}}_{2}=\phi
, hence
\frac{3n}{4}\leq|{\mathfrak{N}}|\leq\frac{4n}{5}
. For any two
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}\neq\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}
, we have

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{z}_{i}\neq\widetilde{z}^{\prime}_{i})\leq\frac{n-|{\mathfrak{N}}|}{n}\leq\frac{1}{4}

This also implies that
h(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z},\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{z}_{i}\neq\widetilde{z}^{\prime}_{i})
. Now define

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}^{(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})}:=\Big\{\left(\mathbf{z},\{\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{2}\},\{\bm{\sigma}_{1},\bm{\sigma}_{2}\}\right):\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}\Big\}

Hence we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{}\mathbf{z}}\sup_{\mathbf{\Theta}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})}}\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{*})\geq\inf_{\widehat{}\mathbf{z}}\sup_{\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}^{(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})}}\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\inf_{\widehat{}\mathbf{z}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}|}\sum_{\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}}\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{N}}^{c}}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq z_{i}^{*})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{N}}^{c}}\inf_{\widehat{z}_{i}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}|}\sum_{\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq z_{i}^{*}),

where the second inequality holds since minimax risk is lower bounded by Bayes risk if we assume a uniform prior on
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}
. It suffices to lower bound the last term.

Now fix any
i\in{\mathfrak{N}}^{c}
, let
\mathbf{Z}^{m}_{\mathfrak{N}}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}:z_{i}^{*}=m\}
for
m=1,2
. Then
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}=\mathbf{Z}^{1}_{\mathfrak{N}}\bigcup\mathbf{Z}^{2}_{\mathfrak{N}}
and
|\mathbf{Z}^{1}_{\mathfrak{N}}|=|\mathbf{Z}^{2}_{\mathfrak{N}}|
by symmetry. This implies that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{z}_{i}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}|}\sum_{\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq z_{i}^{*})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\widehat{z}_{i}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{Z}_{\mathfrak{N}}|}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathbf{Z}^{1}_{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq z_{i}^{*})+\sum_{\mathbf{z}^{*}\in\mathbf{Z}^{2}_{\mathfrak{N}}}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq z_{i}^{*})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\inf_{\widehat{z}_{i}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}^{(i)}}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq 1)+\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}^{(i)}}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq 2)\right),

where we define the following hypothesis testing for each
i\in[n]
:

H_{0}^{(i)}:z_{i}^{*}=1\quad v.s.\quad H_{1}^{(i)}:z_{i}^{*}=2.

By Neyman-Pearson Lemma (see, e.g., Lemma A.2 in
Chen et al. 2022
), the optimal test that minimize the Type-I error
+
Type-II error of above simple v.s. simple hypothesis is given by the likelihood ratio test, which rejects
H_{0}
if

\prod_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)^{\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega)}\left(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\right)^{1-\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega)}>\prod_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)^{\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega)}\left(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\right)^{1-\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega)}

Rearranging terms we obtain

\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}

We need the following lemma, whose proof is relegated to Section
8
.

Lemma 7
.

If
\max_{\omega}\mathbf{P}_{m_{1}}(j_{1},j_{2})/\mathbf{P}_{m_{2}}(j_{1},j_{2})=O(1)
for
\forall m_{1},m_{2}\in[2]
and
\mathbf{X}
is sampled from
\mathbf{P}_{1}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{X}(\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ind.}}}{{\sim}}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\right)\geq\exp\left(-I^{*}(1+o(1))\right),

provided that
I^{*}\rightarrow\infty
.

Now it suffices to apply Lemma
7
to get that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{}\mathbf{z}}\sup_{\mathbf{\Theta}_{\mathbf{z}}^{(\mathbf{B}_{1,2},\bm{\sigma}_{1,2})}}\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{*})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i\in{\mathfrak{N}}^{c}}\inf_{\widehat{z}_{i}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}^{(i)}}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq 1)+\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}^{(i)}}(\widehat{z}_{i}\neq 2)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-I^{*}(1+o(1))\right).

7.2
Proof of Lemma
2

By definition,
\mathbb{I}(\widehat{z}=2)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\left(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{I}\left(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}D_{KL}\left(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)||\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\right)-\delta I^{*}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\left(\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}-\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\Bigg[\log\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}-\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\Bigg]+\delta I^{*}\Bigg),

(31)

where
\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega):=\mathbf{X}(\omega)-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)
and
\delta=o(1)
shall be determined later. We now analyze both terms of (
40
). First notice that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}D_{KL}\left(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)||\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\right)-\delta I^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bigg[1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)+\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}\right)\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}D_{KL}\big(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)||\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\big)+\frac{1}{2}\delta I^{*}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{I^{*}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\delta I^{*}\right)=\exp\bigg(-\frac{I^{*}}{2}(1-o(1))\bigg)

(32)

where the first inequality holds by applying Chernoff bound and the last equality holds provided that
\delta=o(1)
. Denote

\xi_{ideal}:=\mathbb{I}\left(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}D_{KL}\left(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)||\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\right)-\delta I^{*}\right)

Then (
7.2
) implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\xi_{ideal}\leq\exp\bigg(-\frac{I^{*}}{2}(1-o(1))\bigg)

(33)

as
I^{*}\rightarrow\infty
.

It suffices to show the second term of (
40
) vanishes. Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\left(\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}-\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}\right)

\displaystyle>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\Bigg[\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}-\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\Bigg]+\delta I^{*}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\left(\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}+\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}+\log\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}+\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\right)

\displaystyle>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\Bigg[\log\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}+\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\Bigg]+\delta I^{*}\Bigg)

(34)

Without loss of generality, suppose that
\|\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}-\mathbf{P}_{1}\|_{\ell_{1}},\|\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}-\mathbf{P}_{2}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}
for some
\tilde{\rho}=o(1)
. This means that for
\forall m\in[1,2]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}|\mathbf{P}_{m}(\omega)-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}(\omega)|\leq\tilde{\rho}I^{*}

This implies that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\Bigg[\log\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}+\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\Bigg]\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\Bigg|\log\left(1+\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\right)\Bigg|+\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\Bigg|\log\left(1+\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\right)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\left|\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\right|(1+o(1))+\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\left|\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\right|(1+o(1))

\displaystyle\lesssim\tilde{\rho}I^{*}

Now set
\delta=\tilde{\rho}^{\epsilon}
for some fixed
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and we get

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\Bigg[\log\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}+\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\Bigg]\Bigg|=o\left(\delta I^{*}\right).

Then term (
7.2
) can be further bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\left(\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}-\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}\right)

\displaystyle>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\Bigg[\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}-\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\Bigg]+\delta I^{*}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}>\frac{\delta}{8}I^{*}\Bigg)+\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}>\frac{\delta}{8}I^{*}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}>\frac{\delta}{8}I^{*}\Bigg)+\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}>\frac{\delta}{8}I^{*}\Bigg)

(35)

To control the first term of (
7.2
), we begin with:

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\left|\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\frac{|\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)|}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}(1+o(1))

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}

(36)

where in the first inequality we’ve used the following fact:

\displaystyle\frac{I^{*}}{d(d+1)/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{d(d+1)/2}\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\frac{\left(\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)\right)^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\vee\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{d(d+1)/2}\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\vee\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)

\displaystyle\lesssim\min_{\omega}\min_{m\in[2]}\mathbf{P}_{m}(\omega),

(37)

where the last inequality holds by the constraints in the parameter space
\mathbf{\Theta}
in (
2.1
) and the block structures of
\mathbf{P}_{m}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}
so that
|\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)|/\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)=O(1)
.

Therefore there exists an absolute constant
C\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\bigg(\sum_{\omega}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}>\frac{\delta}{8}I^{*}\bigg)\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\bigg(\sum_{\omega}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}>\frac{\delta}{8C}I^{*}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{I}\bigg(\sum_{\omega}\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}>\frac{\delta I^{\ast}}{16C}\bigg)

where
\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega):=\mathbf{X}(\omega)-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)
and we used the fact
\delta=\rho^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Denote the event

{\cal G}:=\left\{\sum_{\omega}\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}>\frac{\delta I^{\ast}}{16C}\right\}.

Due to independence between
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{X}
, we have, by Chernoff bound and conditioned on
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}
, that for any
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\cal G}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda\delta}{16C}I^{*}\right)\prod_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbb{E}\exp\bigg(\lambda\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\bigg)

(38)

By choosing
\lambda\asymp\tilde{\rho}^{-\varepsilon}
so that
\lambda\tilde{\rho}=o(1)
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\lambda\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\exp\left(\lambda\big(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\big)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\right)+(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))\exp\bigg(-\lambda\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\left(\exp\left(\lambda\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\right)-1\right)\right)\exp\left(-\lambda\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{\ast}/d^{2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\leq}\left(1+2\frac{\lambda\tilde{\rho}I^{*}}{d^{2}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda\tilde{\rho}I^{*}}{d^{2}}\right)\overset{(b)}{\leq}\exp\left(\frac{\lambda\tilde{\rho}I^{*}}{d^{2}}\right)

where in (a) we’ve used (
7.2
) and
e^{x}\leq 1+2x
for
0<x<1
, in (b) we’ve used
e^{x}\geq 1+x
for
x>0
. Recall that
\delta=\tilde{\rho}^{\epsilon}
for some
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, (
38
) can be further bounded as

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda\delta}{16}I^{*}\right)\prod_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbb{E}\left(\exp\left(\lambda\bar{}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\frac{\frac{\tilde{\rho}I^{*}}{d(d+1)/2}}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\lambda\tilde{\rho}I^{*}-\frac{\lambda\delta}{16}I^{*}\right)\leq\exp\left(-c\lambda\delta I^{*}/2\right)

for some absolute constant
c>0
.
Then we can choose
\lambda=(c\delta)^{-1}=c^{-1}\tilde{\rho}^{-\epsilon}
and obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left({\cal G}\right)\leq\exp(-I^{*}/2).

The same high probability bounds for the other three terms of (
7.2
) can be obtained similarly and hence omitted. Then

\displaystyle\xi_{pertub}:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\Bigg(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}(\omega)\left(\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}-\log\frac{\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\Bigg[\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}-\log\frac{1-\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\Bigg]+\delta I^{*}\Bigg)

Hence we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\xi_{pertub}\leq 4\exp\left(-I^{*}/2\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{I^{*}}{2}(1-o(1))\right)

(39)

7.3
Proof of Theorem
2

We introduce extra notations for convenience. For any
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in[2]^{n}
and
\bm{\sigma},\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}:[d]\rightarrow[K]
, we define

h_{0}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\left(z_{i}\neq z^{\prime}_{i}\right)\quad{\rm and}\quad h_{0}(\bm{\sigma},\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{I}\left(\bm{\sigma}(j)\neq\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}(j)\right)

For each
l\in[n]
, by definition there exists some
\pi_{(-l)}\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}
and
\phi_{(-l)}\in\mathfrak{S}_{K}
such that
h_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-l)},\pi_{(-l)}(\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\right)=h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-l)},\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{z}
and
h_{0}\left(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{(-l)},\phi_{(-l)}\circ\bm{\sigma}\right)=h\left(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{(-l)},\bm{\sigma}^{\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{\sigma}
.

We now fix an
i\in[n]
and suppose
z_{i}^{\ast}=1
. Without loss of generality, we assume
\pi_{(-i)}=\text{Id}
and
\phi_{(-i)}=\text{Id}
2
2

2

Otherwise we can always replace
\mathbf{P}_{m}(j_{1},j_{2})
,
\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)
,
\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j)
with
\mathbf{P}_{\pi(m)}(j_{1},j_{2})
,
\mathbf{B}_{\pi}(m)(\phi(k),\phi(l))
,
\phi\circ\bm{\sigma}_{\pi(m)}(j)
in the following analysis.
. To avoid clutters of notations, we temporarily drop the superscript
(-i)
in
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}^{(-i)}(j_{1},j_{2})
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{m}^{(-i)}(k,l)
but keep in mind the independence structure between
\mathbf{X}_{i}
and other estimated parameters. Similarly as Lemma
2
, the
i
-th network is mis-clustered when

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\big(\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}=2\big)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\left(\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\mathbf{X}_{i}(\omega)\log\frac{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega))}{\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)(1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega))}>\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\log\frac{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}(\omega)}{1-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}(\omega)}\right).

(40)

Since
\mathbf{X}_{i}
is independent of
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{1}^{(-i)}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{2}^{(-i)}
, it suffices to apply Lemma
2
. Towards that end, we must bound the entry-wise error of
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}^{(-i)}_{1}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{P}^{(-i)}_{2}
, respectively. It thus suffices to bound the error of
\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{(-i)}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{(-i)}
, respectively, as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 8
.

Under the same conditions of Theorem
2
, there exists a large absolute constant
C>0
such that, for each
i\in[n]
, with probability at least
1-n^{-C}
, we have

\displaystyle\min_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{2},\phi\in\mathfrak{S}_{K}}\max_{m\in\{1,2\}}\max_{k,l\in[K]}\left|\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{m}^{(-i)}(k,l)-\mathbf{B}_{\pi(m)}(\phi(k),\phi(l))\right|=\tilde{\rho}\cdot\frac{I^{*}}{d(d+1)/2}

(41)

with some
\tilde{\rho}=o(1)
.

Denote the event
{\cal A}_{(-i)}:=\{\eqref{eq:initial-error-event}\text{~holds}\}
. Notice that
\mathbb{P}({\cal A}_{(-i)})\geq 1-n^{-C}
and we now proceed conditioned on
{\cal A}_{(-i)}
. Observe that, for
m\in\{1,2\}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}

\displaystyle|\mathbf{P}_{m}(\omega)-\widehat{}\mathbf{P}_{m}(\omega)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bigg|\sum_{k,l\in[K]}\left[\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)\mathbb{I}\big(\bm{\sigma}_{m}(\omega)=(k,l)\big)-\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)\mathbb{I}\big(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}(\omega)=(k,l)\big)\right]\bigg|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\omega\in{\cal J}_{d}}\bigg|\sum_{k,l\in[K]}\bigg[\left(\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)-\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)\right)\mathbb{I}\big(\bm{\sigma}_{m}(\omega)=(k,l)\big)\mathbb{I}\big(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}(\omega)=\bm{\sigma}_{m}(\omega)\big)

\displaystyle-\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)\mathbb{I}\big(\bm{\sigma}_{m}(\omega)\neq(k,l)\big)\mathbb{I}\big(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}(\omega)\neq\bm{\sigma}_{m}(\omega)\big)\bigg]\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\omega}\max_{k,l\in[K]}\Big|\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)-\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)\Big|+2K^{2}\max_{k,l\in[K]}\widehat{}\mathbf{B}_{m}(k,l)\sum_{\omega}\mathbb{I}\big(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}(\omega)\neq\bm{\sigma}_{m}(\omega)\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\tilde{\rho}I^{*}+K^{2}d^{2}\eta_{\sigma}\cdot\frac{\bar{a}}{(n\wedge d)d}\lesssim\tilde{\rho}I^{*},

where we’ve used (
59
) and
\eta_{\sigma}=o\left(\frac{I^{*}}{\bar{a}K^{2}}\left(\frac{n}{d}\wedge 1\right)\right)
. By Lemma
2
, we conclude, conditioned on the event
{\cal A}_{(-i)}
and the event defined in (
8
), that

\mathbb{P}(\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}\neq z_{i}^{\ast})\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{I^{\ast}}{2}\big(1-o(1)\big)\Big)

Now we need the following lemma to validate our final step for label alignments, which is a special case of
(
Gao et al. 2017
, Lemma 4)
. Recall that
{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z})=\{i\in[n]:z_{i}=m\}
for each
m\in[2]
and any
\mathbf{z}\in[2]^{n}
.

Lemma 9

(
Gao et al. 2017
)
.

For any label vector
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}^{n}
such that
|{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z})|\wedge|{\cal I}_{m}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|\geq\frac{n}{2\alpha}
and
h(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})\leq c\alpha^{-1}

for some absolute constant
c\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
, define the map
\varsigma:[2]\rightarrow[2]
as

\displaystyle\varsigma(k):=\argmax_{m\in\{1,2\}}\left|\left\{j\in[n]:z_{j}=m\right\}\bigcap\left\{j\in[n]:z_{j}^{\prime}=k\right\}\right|,\quad k\in\{1,2\}

Then we have
\varsigma\in S_{2}
and
h_{0}\left(\mathbf{z},\varsigma(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})\right)=h\left(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\right)
.

Notice that after refinement procedure for all samples
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, we can obtain
n
label vectors
\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}
, each of which only differs from
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}
at the
i
-th label. Since for any
i\in[n]
,
h_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)},\pi_{(-i)}(\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\right)=h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)},\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{z}
. Hence we have

\displaystyle h_{0}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)},\pi_{(-i)}(\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\right)\leq\eta_{z}+\frac{1}{n}

(42)

For each
i=2,\cdots,n
, define the map
\varsigma_{(-i)}:[2]\rightarrow[2]
as

\displaystyle\varsigma_{(-i)}(k):=\argmax_{m\in\{1,2\}}\left|\left\{j\in[n]:\widehat{z}_{j}^{(-1)}=m\right\}\bigcap\left\{j\in[n]:\widehat{z}_{j}^{(-i)}=k\right\}\right|,\quad k\in\{1,2\}

By definition, we have
\widehat{z}_{i}=\varsigma_{(-i)}\left(\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}\right)
. We can assume
\pi_{(-1)}=\text{Id}
without loss of generality, then (
42
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle h_{0}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-1)},\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{z}+\frac{1}{n},\quad h_{0}\left(\pi_{(-i)}^{-1}(\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}),\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{z}+\frac{1}{n}

We therefore have
h_{0}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-1)},\pi^{-1}_{(-i)}(\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)})\right)\leq h_{0}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-1)},\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)+h_{0}\left(\pi_{(-i)}^{-1}(\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}),\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\leq 2\eta_{z}+\frac{2}{n}=o(1)
. Then we can apply Lemma
9
with
\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-1)}
and
\widehat{}\mathbf{z}^{(-i)}
, and obtain that
\varsigma_{(-i)}=\pi^{-1}_{(-i)}
for
i=2,\cdots,n
. Thus we have

\displaystyle h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})

\displaystyle\leq h_{0}(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\left(\pi^{-1}_{(-i)}\left(\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}\right)\neq z_{i}^{\ast}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\left(\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}\neq\pi_{(-i)}\left(z_{i}^{\ast}\right)\right)

By the above relation and eq. (
33
), eq. (
39
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\mathbb{I}\left(\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}\neq\pi_{(-i)}\left(z_{i}^{\ast}\right)\right)\mathbb{I}\left({\cal A}_{(-i)}\right)+\mathbb{E}\mathbb{I}\left(\widehat{z}_{i}^{(-i)}\neq\pi_{(-i)}\left(z_{i}^{\ast}\right)\right)\mathbb{I}\left({\cal A}^{c}_{(-i)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\bigg(-\frac{I^{*}}{2}(1-o(1))\bigg)+n^{-C}

By Markov’s inequality, we further obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\geq\exp((I^{*}/2)^{1-\epsilon})\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\right)\leq\exp\left(-(I^{*}/2)^{1-\epsilon}\right)

for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, which implies that with probability at least
1-\exp\left(-(I^{*}/2)^{1-\epsilon}\right)
,

\displaystyle h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\big((I^{\ast}/2)^{1-\epsilon}\big)\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\bigg(-\frac{I^{*}}{2}(1-o(1))\bigg)+\exp\left((I^{*}/2)^{1-\epsilon}\right)n^{-C}

(43)

as
I^{*}\rightarrow\infty
. Finally, it suffices to notice that
h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})<n^{-1}
implying
h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})=0
, thus
n^{-C}
is ignorable and
n^{-C}<\exp\left(-(1-o(1))I^{\ast}/2\right)
. We thus conclude, under the event of (
8
), that

\displaystyle h(\widehat{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\exp\bigg(-\frac{I^{*}}{2}(1-o(1))\bigg),

(44)

with probability at least
1-\exp(-(I^{*}/2)^{1-\epsilon})
.

7.4
Proof of Theorem
3

The proof is proceeded with several steps. Without loss of generality, we assume
{\cal N}_{0}=\{1,\cdots,n_{0}\}
and
{\cal V}_{0}=\{1,\cdots,d_{0}\}
throughout the proof.

Lower bound on core tensor and incoherent condition.

Under conditions of Theorem
3
, Lemma 4.1 in
Jing et al. 2021
implies that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}(\bar{}\mathbf{C})\gtrsim\kappa_{0}^{-2}r^{-1}\sqrt{n}d\bar{p},

and Lemma 4.2 in
Jing et al. 2021
implies that

\displaystyle\delta=\left\|\bar{}\mathbf{U}\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq\kappa_{0}\sqrt{\frac{r}{d}}

The above two inequalities will be used repeatedly in the proof.

Sample-splitting properties

Due to sample splitting, it is more convenient to view the sets
{\cal N}^{[0]},{\cal N}^{[1]},{\cal V}^{[0]},{\cal V}^{[1]}
as fixed. In fact, by Hoeffding’s inequality we have with probability at least
1-\left(n\wedge d\right)^{-10}
, there exists some absolute constant
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle{\left(1-c_{1}\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\frac{n_{m}^{\ast}}{2}}<\left|\left\{i\in{\cal N}^{[k]}:z_{i}^{\ast}=m\right\}\right|\leq\left(1+c_{1}\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\frac{n_{m}^{\ast}}{2},

\displaystyle\left(1-c_{2}\frac{\log d}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\frac{d_{m,l}}{2}<\left|\left\{j\in{\cal V}^{[k]}:\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j)=l\right\}\right|\leq\left(1+c_{2}\frac{\log d}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\frac{d_{m,l}}{2}

for
k\in[2]
,
m\in[2]
and
l\in[K]
, where we define
d_{m,l}:=|S_{l}(\bm{\sigma}_{m})|
. Moreover, denote
n_{m}^{[k]\ast}:=\left|\left\{i\in{\cal N}^{[k]}:z_{i}^{\ast}=m\right\}\right|
and for
i\in{\cal N}^{[k]}
, denote
\mathbf{P}^{[+,k]}_{m}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}\times d_{k}}
to be the sub-matrix of
\mathbf{P}_{m}
restricting to the vertices in
{\cal V}^{[k]}
.

Notice that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{min}}\left(n_{1}^{[0]\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{1}+n_{2}^{[0]\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{2}\right)=\sigma_{\text{min}}\left(\left(1+O\left(\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{1}+\left(1+O\left(\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\text{min}}\left(n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{1}+n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{2}+C_{1}\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{1}+C_{2}\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{\geq}\sigma_{\text{min}}\left(n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{1}+n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{2}\right)-\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\left\|C_{1}n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{1}+C_{2}n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{2}\right\|

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{\geq}\sigma_{\text{min}}\left(n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}_{1}+n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}_{2}\right)-\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\left\|C_{1}n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}_{1}+C_{2}n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}_{2}\right\|

\displaystyle\geq C\sqrt{nd\bar{p}}-C^{\prime}\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot nd\bar{p}\overset{(c)}{\geq}\widetilde{C}\sqrt{nd\bar{p}}

where in (a) we’ve used Wely’s inequality, in (b) we’ve used that
n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}_{1}^{[+,0]}+n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}_{2}^{[+,0]}
is a principle sub-matrix of
n_{1}^{\star}\mathbf{P}_{1}+n_{2}^{\star}\mathbf{P}_{2}
and hence the Cauchy interlacing theorem applies, in (c) we’ve used that
\bar{p}\lesssim\left(d\log^{2}n\right)^{-1}
which is further implied by
\bar{a}\lesssim\left(n\wedge d\right)/\log^{2}n
.
Due to the independence of sample splitting and the data itself, we assume the above results holds deterministically.

Pre-estimation for reference labels.

For the performance of pre-estimation
\check{}\mathbf{z}
and
\left(\check{\bm{\sigma}}_{1},\check{\bm{\sigma}}_{2}\right)
of RSpec (i.e., Algorithm
2
), we have the following result.

Proposition 1
.

With probability at least
1-(n\vee d)^{-3}
, the output of Algorithm
2
satisfies

\displaystyle h(\check{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\leq\frac{C\kappa_{0}^{6}r^{3}\log(\kappa_{0}r)\log^{4}(n\vee d)}{\bar{a}}

and

\displaystyle\max_{m\in\{1,2\}}h(\check{\bm{\sigma}}_{m},\bm{\sigma}_{m})\leq C\left(\frac{\log^{6}(n\vee d)}{\bar{a}}+\left(\frac{\kappa_{0}^{6}r^{3}\log(\kappa_{0}r)\log^{4}(n\vee d)}{\bar{a}}\right)^{2}\right)

for some absolute constant
C>0
, provided that
\bar{a}\gg\kappa_{0}^{6}r^{3}\log(\kappa_{0}r)\log^{6}(n\vee d)
.

Sample-switching estimation for network labels.

Without loss of generality we first consider
k=1,k^{\prime}=0
i.e.,

\displaystyle\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}\longleftarrow\text{SVD}_{r}\left(\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{[+]}\right),\quad\widehat{}\mathbf{W}^{[1]}\longleftarrow\text{SVD}_{2}\left({\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+}^{[1]}\times_{1}{\cal P}_{\delta_{1}}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}\right)\times_{2}{\cal P}_{\delta_{1}}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}\right)\right)\right),

and
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]}\longleftarrow
K-means clustering on rows of
\widehat{}\mathbf{W}^{[1]}
.

We first upper bound
\min_{\mathbf{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{r}}\left\|\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}-\bar{}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{O}\right\|
. Here we slightly abuse the notation to denote
\bar{}\mathbf{U}
the corresponding left singular vectors of the sub-networks either on
{\cal V}_{1}
and
{\cal V}_{2}
and, respectively,
d
can be
d_{0}
and
d_{1}
.
By Lemma
14
and Davis-Kahan theorem, we obtain that with probability at least
1-(n\vee d)^{-3}
,

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{r}}\left\|\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}-\bar{}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{O}\right\|\leq\frac{C_{0}\sqrt{nd\bar{p}}}{\sigma_{r}\left(\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}}\mathbb{E}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{[+]}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{4}

provided that
\sigma_{r}\left(n_{1}^{[0]\ast}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{1}+n_{2}^{[0]\ast}\mathbf{P}^{[+,0]}_{2}\right)\geq 4C_{0}\sqrt{nd\bar{p}}
. Denote
\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}:={\cal P}_{\delta_{1}}\left(\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}\right)
. By the property of regularization operator
{\cal P}_{\delta}
(see
(
Jing et al. 2021
, Theorem 5.1)
), we have

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{r}}\left\|\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}-\bar{}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{O}\right\|\leq 2\sqrt{2}\cdot\min_{\mathbf{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{r}}\left\|\widehat{}\mathbf{U}^{[0]}-\bar{}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{O}\right\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq\sqrt{2}\delta

(45)

It turns out that we need to upper bound
\left\|{\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left(\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{[1]}_{+}\times_{1}\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\times_{2}\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\right)\right\|=\left\|{\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left(\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{[1]}_{+}\right)\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\otimes\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\right)\right\|
with
\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{[1]}_{+}:={\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+}^{[1]}-\mathbb{E}{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{+}^{[1]}
. The following lemma is derived from recent result on matrix concentration inequality
(
Brailovskaya and van Handel 2022
)
, the proof of which is delegated to Section
8
.

Lemma 10
.

Suppose
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d\times n}
is a Bernoulli tensor such that for each
i\in[n]
,
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}(:,:,i)\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d}
is a symmetric Bernoulli matrix with independent entries up to symmetry. Denote
\bar{p}=\left\|\mathbb{E}{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\right\|_{\infty}
. For any
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{O}_{d,r}
such that
\left\|\mathbf{U}\right\|_{2,\infty}\leq C\kappa_{0}\sqrt{\frac{r}{d}}
for some constant
C>0
, then with probability at least
1-(n\vee d)^{-3}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}-\mathbb{E}{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\right)\left(\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}\right)\right\|\leq C\sqrt{n\bar{p}},

provided that
n/\log n\geq C_{1}\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{6}
and
\sqrt{nd^{2}\bar{p}}\geq C_{2}(\kappa_{0}r)^{2}\log^{2}(n\vee d)
for some absolute constant
C_{1},C_{2}>0
.

Due to the independence of
\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}
and of
\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{[1]}_{+}
, we can view
\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}
as fixed (or equivalently, conditional on
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[0]}_{+}
) and apply Lemma
10
(note the conditions in Lemma
10
is met automatically under our setting) to obtain that

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left(\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{[1]}_{+}\right)\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\otimes\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\right)\right\|\lesssim\sqrt{n\bar{p}}

with probability at least
1-(n\vee d)^{-3}
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{r}\left({\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left(\mathbb{E}{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[1]}_{+}\right)\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\otimes\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\right)\right)=\sigma_{r}\left({\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left(\bar{}\mathbf{C}\right)\left(\bar{}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\otimes\bar{}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\right)\right)\geq\sigma_{r}\left({\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left(\bar{}\mathbf{C}\right)\right)\sigma^{2}_{\min}\left(\bar{}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\right)\geq\frac{\sigma_{r}\left({\mathscr{M}}_{3}\left(\bar{}\mathbf{C}\right)\right)}{4}

with probability at least
1-(n\vee d)^{-3}
, where we’ve used the fact

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{I}-\bar{}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\bar{}\mathbf{U}\right\|\leq\min_{\mathbf{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{d,r}}\left\|\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}-\bar{}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{O}\right\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}

by (
45
) and hence
\sigma_{\min}\left(\bar{}\mathbf{U}^{\top}\widetilde{}\mathbf{U}\right)\geq 1/2
. We then obtain, by Davis-Kahan theorem, that with the same probability,

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{O}\in\mathbb{O}_{2}}\left\|\widehat{}\mathbf{W}^{[1]}-\bar{}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{O}\right\|\leq\frac{4C\sqrt{n\bar{p}}}{\sigma_{\min}(\bar{}\mathbf{C})}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}\kappa_{0}^{2}r}{\sqrt{d^{2}\bar{p}},}

Following the same argument in the proof of first claim in Theorem 5.4 in
Jing et al. 2021
, we obtain

\displaystyle h(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]},\mathbf{z}^{[1]\ast})\leq\frac{C\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{2}}{d^{2}\bar{p}}\leq\frac{C\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{2}}{\bar{a}},

with probability at least
1-(n\vee d)^{-3}
.

By symmetry, we can similarly obtain the bound for
h(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})
by considering
k=0,k^{\prime}=1
. We thus conclude that with probability at least
1-3(n\vee d)^{-3}
, we have

\displaystyle h(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[k]},\mathbf{z}^{[k]\ast})\leq\frac{C\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{2}}{d^{2}\bar{p}}\leq\frac{C\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{2}}{\bar{a}},\quad\forall k\in\{0,1\}

(46)

Sample-switching estimation for local memberships.

The proof strategy in this part is similar to the proof of Proposition
1
. We first consider
k=0,k^{\prime}=1
and
m\in\{1,2\}
. Recall
\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{m}^{[1]}
is obtained by K-means clustering on rows of
\text{SVD}_{K}\left(\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}:\widetilde{z}_{i}^{[0]}=m}\mathbf{X}^{[-]}_{i}\right)
. Without loss of generality, we assume
h_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)=h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)
(see the definition of
h_{0}(\cdot)
in the proof Theorem
2
). For
i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}
, denote
\mathbf{E}_{i}^{[-]}:=\mathbf{X}_{i}^{[-]}-\mathbf{P}^{[-,0]}_{z_{i}^{\ast}}
, where
\mathbf{P}^{[-,0]}_{z_{i}^{\ast}}=\mathbb{E}\mathbf{X}^{[-]}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}}
is the expectation of sub-matrix of
\mathbf{X}_{i}
restricting to the vertices in
{\cal V}^{[1]}
and
\widetilde{n}^{[0]}_{m}:=\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}}\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{z}_{i}^{[0]}=m)
for
m\in\{1,2\}
. Let
{\cal A}
denote the event in (
46
) we continue on
{\cal A}
. For
m\in\{1,2\}
we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{n}^{[0]}_{m}=\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}}\mathbb{I}\left(\widetilde{z}_{i}^{[0]}=m\right)\geq\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}}\mathbb{I}\left(z_{i}^{\ast}=m\right)-\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}}\mathbb{I}\left(z_{i}^{\ast}\neq\widetilde{z}^{[0]}_{i}\right)\geq\frac{c_{1}n}{2}-nh(\check{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast})\gtrsim n_{m}^{[0]\ast}

where the last inequality holds if
\bar{a}\gg\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{2}
. Following the same argument line by line of the proof of Proposition
1
(local memberships estimation part), it suffices for us to bound

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{m}^{[0]\ast}}\left\|\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}}\mathbb{I}\left(\widetilde{z}_{i}^{[0]}\neq m,z_{i}^{\ast}=m\right)\mathbf{E}_{i}^{[-]}\right\|

(47)

Now since
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}
is obtained by using
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[0]}_{+}
and
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}^{[1]}_{+}
, we have independence between
\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}
and
\mathbf{E}_{i}^{[-]}
. Instead of using Lemma
15
, we can sharply control the concentration of (
47
) by Lemma
14
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{m}^{[0]\ast}}\left\|\sum_{i\in{\cal N}^{[0]}}\mathbb{I}\left(\widetilde{z}_{i}^{[0]}\neq m,z_{i}^{\ast}=m\right)\mathbf{E}_{i}^{[-]}\right\|\lesssim\frac{1}{n_{m}^{[0]\ast}}\sqrt{n_{m}^{[0]\ast}h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)d\bar{p}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d\bar{p}}{n}}

with probability at least
1-(n\vee d)^{-3}
. Define
\bm{\sigma}_{m}^{[k]}(j)=\bm{\sigma}_{m}(j)
for
j\in{\cal V}^{[k]}
and
k=0,1
, the same argument of the proof of Proposition
1
would lead to

\displaystyle h\left(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{[1]}_{m},\bm{\sigma}_{m}^{[1]}\right)\leq\frac{\frac{d\bar{p}}{n}+h^{2}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)\cdot(d\bar{p})^{2}}{\sigma_{K}^{2}\left(\mathbf{P}^{[-,0]}_{m}\right)}\lesssim\frac{1}{\bar{a}}

with probability at least
1-3(n\vee d)^{-3}
, where we’ve used
\sigma_{K}^{2}\left(\mathbf{P}^{[-,0]}_{m}\right)\gtrsim\left(d\bar{p}\right)^{2}
and
\bar{a}\gg\kappa_{0}^{8}r^{4}\log(\kappa_{0}r)\log^{6}(n\vee d)
. By symmetry, the same argument applies to the case when
k=1,k^{\prime}=0
. We can thereby conclude that

\displaystyle\max_{m\in\{1,2\}}h\left(\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{[k]}_{m},\bm{\sigma}^{[k]}_{m}\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{\bar{a}},\quad\forall k\in\{0,1\}

with probability exceeding
1-C(n\vee d)^{-3}
.

Alignment for network labels.

Denote the event

\displaystyle{\cal A}:=\left\{h\left(\check{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{1}\text{~and~}h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[k]},\mathbf{z}^{[k]\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{2}\text{~for~}k=0,1\right\}

where

\eta_{1}={C\kappa_{0}^{6}r^{3}\log(\kappa_{0}r)\log^{4}(n\vee d)}(\bar{a})^{-1}
and
\eta_{2}={C\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{2}}(\bar{a})^{-1}
.
According to our previous analysis, we have
\mathbb{P}({\cal A})\geq 1-3(n\vee d)^{-3}
and we proceed on
{\cal A}
. Denote
\mathbf{z}^{\ast\top}=(\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast\top},\mathbf{z}^{[1]\ast\top})
and
\check{}\mathbf{z}^{\top}=(\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]\top},\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]\top})
. By definition, there exists some
\check{\pi}\in S_{2}
such that
h_{0}\left(\check{}\mathbf{z},\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\right)=h\left(\check{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)
. Hence we have

h_{0}\left(\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)\right)=h\left(\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{1}
and
h_{0}\left(\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]},\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{[1]\ast}\right)\right)=h\left(\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]},\mathbf{z}^{[1]\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{1}
.
On the other hand, there exists some
\widetilde{\pi}_{0},\widetilde{\pi}_{1}\in S_{2}
such that
h_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\widetilde{\pi}_{0}\left(\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)\right)=h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{2}
and
h_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]},\widetilde{\pi}_{1}\left(\mathbf{z}^{[1]\ast}\right)\right)=h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]},\mathbf{z}^{[1]\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{2}
. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle h_{0}\left(\widetilde{\pi}_{0}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\check{\pi}^{-1}\left(\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)\right)\leq h_{0}\left(\check{\pi}^{-1}\left(\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)+h_{0}\left(\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)\leq\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}

Hence
h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)\leq h_{0}\left(\check{\pi}\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{0}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)\leq\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=o(1)
with the proviso that
a\gg\kappa_{0}^{6}r^{3}\log^{4}(n\vee d)
. This indicates that
h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)=h_{0}\left(\check{\pi}\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{0}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)
.

Now for
k\in\{0,1\}
, define the map
\varsigma_{k}:[2]\rightarrow[2]
as

\displaystyle\varsigma_{k}(m)=\argmax_{m^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}}\left|\left\{j\in[n_{k}]:\check{z}_{j}^{[k]}=m^{\prime}\right\}\bigcap\left\{j\in[n_{k}]:\widetilde{z}_{j}^{[k]}=m\right\}\right|,\quad m=1,2

By definition we have
\widetilde{z}_{i}=\varsigma_{0}\left(\widetilde{z}_{i}^{[0]}\right)
for
i=1,\cdots,n_{0}
. By Lemma
9
and the fact that
h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)=o(1)
, we conclude that
\varsigma_{0}=\check{\pi}\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{0}^{-1}
. That is to say,
h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)=h_{0}\left(\varsigma_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)\leq\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=o(1)
. Meanwhile, we have

\displaystyle h_{0}\left(\varsigma_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\ast[0]}\right)\right)\leq h_{0}\left(\varsigma_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right)+h_{0}\left(\check{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\ast[0]}\right)\right)\leq 2\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=o(1)

Repeating the same argument, we can obtain
h_{0}\left(\varsigma_{1}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]}\right),\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\ast[1]}\right)\right)\leq 2\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}=o(1)
with
\varsigma_{1}=\check{\pi}\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{1}^{-1}
. This implies that

\displaystyle h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)

\displaystyle\leq h_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z},\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\right)\leq\frac{n_{0}\cdot h_{0}\left(\varsigma_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]}\right),\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)\right)+n_{1}\cdot h_{0}\left(\varsigma_{1}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]}\right),\check{\pi}\left(\mathbf{z}^{[1]\ast}\right)\right)}{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{n_{0}\cdot h_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[0]},\widetilde{\pi}_{0}\left(\mathbf{z}^{[0]\ast}\right)\right)+n_{1}\cdot h_{0}\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z}^{[1]},\widetilde{\pi}_{1}\left(\mathbf{z}^{[1]\ast}\right)\right)}{n}\leq 2\eta_{2}

In other word, if
\bar{a}\gg\kappa_{0}^{6}r^{3}\log^{2}(n\vee d)
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{h\left(\widetilde{}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\ast}\right)\leq\frac{C\kappa_{0}^{4}r^{2}}{\bar{a}}\right\}\bigcap{\cal A}\right)\geq 1-3(n\vee d)^{-3}.

(48)

Alignment for local memberships.
